# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-22

| **[GBClaw: An agentic AI framework for atomistic grain boundary research and mechanistic discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28629v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic artificial intelligence (AI) systems hold immense potential for automating complex workflows in materials discovery and design, but extending them to domain-intensive mechanistic investigations remains challenging. Here, we present GBClaw, an agentic AI framework for investigating the structure and deformation of general grain boundaries (GBs). Although general GBs constitute the vast majority of real-world interfaces in polycrystalline materials, they remain understudied because their structural complexity and interfacial mismatch complicate experimental and modeling research. GBClaw integrates large language models (LLMs) into a modular architecture comprising an orchestrator, specialized agents, domain-specific tools, simulation interfaces, and hierarchical memory. Through dynamic replanning and iterative workflow refinement, the framework effectively connects systematic simulations with targeted mechanistic analysis. Applied to 14 general asymmetric tilt GBs in nickel, GBClaw characterized interfacial defect structures and identified diverse deformation modes across these incommensurate boundaries. Comparing boundary sliding with and without secondary GB misfit dislocations revealed substantial differences in shear strength, underscoring the critical role of defect hierarchies in sliding resistance. Furthermore, researcher-guided follow-up calculations traced an anomalously high shear resistance to metastable boundary structures. This work demonstrates that combining agentic AI with domain expertise bridges high-throughput computational screening with deep physical understanding of complex material interfaces.</p></details> |  |
| **[TraceLite: Efficient Trace Reduction for SWE Agents via Optical Compression and Redundancy Removal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28612v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Software engineering agents solve repository-level tasks through iterative tool use, but their continuously growing interaction traces incur substantial token consumption and inference overhead. Existing compression approaches often require model training, auxiliary LLM calls, or assumptions about specific content such as source code. We present TraceLite, a simple yet effective, training-free framework for compressing heterogeneous SWE-agent observations. Our preliminary study shows that optical rendering preserves substantial textual and semantic information under moderate compression, while agent trajectories exhibit both cross-step and phase-level redundancy. Motivated by these findings, TraceLite preserves recent observations as high-fidelity text, renders historical observations as compact images, removes overlapping content, and discards phase-expired observation payloads after code editing. It requires neither offline training nor auxiliary LLM inference. We evaluate TraceLite on SWE-bench Verified and DeepSWE using GPT-5.6-Luna and Gemini-3.8Flash as backbone models. On SWE-bench Verified, TraceLite achieves 78.25% resolve rate, outperforming the evaluated compression baselines while reducing average input-token consumption and API cost by 33.05% and 21.30%, respectively. Moreover, it introduces only 0.438 seconds of compression latency per observation. These results demonstrate that TraceLite improves SWE-agent efficiency with low processing overhead and without compromising issue-resolution capability. Our code is available at https://github.com/xjywhu/TraceLite.</p></details> |  |
| **[Evolving Agent Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28601v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Computer-use agents are limited by the interfaces humans give them. An agent may type a spreadsheet cell by cell when a single line of code would do, or print all two thousand lines of a file through a shell to find one function. Both the interfaces and the knowledge of when to use each are built by hand, which cannot keep pace with new applications, workflows, and models. We argue that interface design should itself be an agent capability. We introduce Agent S4, an autonomous computer-use scientist that builds Evolving Agent Interfaces from task experience. A meta-agent examines task agents' trajectories on synthetic training tasks, without success labels, and constructs and revises interfaces and usage knowledge in a repository. Population-based evolution then selects which repositories to develop further by their performance on validation tasks the meta-agent never sees, favoring changes that generalize beyond the seen tasks. On OSWorld V2, Agent S4, with Qwen3.8-27B and the evolved repository of interfaces, sets a new state of the art in open-source computer-use agents, reaching 21.3% binary score, 3.8 times the same model with fixed interfaces. This is above both GPT-5.5 (13.0%) and Claude Opus 4.8 (20.6%), at about 4.3% and 1.5% of their respective costs. On ScienceBoard, the evolved interfaces improve the success rate from 41.4% to 58.6% with the same model. These results suggest that useful interfaces can be evolved from experience, making autonomous interface design a source of improvement complementary to advances in models and harnesses. We open-source our code at https://github.com/simular-ai/agent-s.</p></details> |  |
| **[AgenticRoboRL: Bringing Coding-Agent Post-Training to Agentic Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28589v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic robotics systems combine planners with robot harnesses to perform complex manipulation tasks. Recent work improves these systems through better harnesses and external memory, but relies on powerful closed-source models as planners and does not improve the planners' own capabilities. In contrast, coding agents have improved substantially by post-training the model itself. We therefore investigate whether post-training can also improve robot planners. However, applying it across robot harnesses requires accommodating different execution mechanisms, including VLA skill invocation and control-code generation. To this end, we introduce AgenticRoboRL, a framework for post-training robot planners across different harnesses. The training recipe combines supervised fine-tuning with multi-turn reinforcement learning. To accommodate different execution mechanisms, harness-specific adapters connect recorded planner interactions with task outcomes, enabling a common RL training pipeline while keeping execution components fixed. On LIBERO-PRO, AgenticRoboRL raises the success rate of a 9B-parameter planner from 18.1% to 71.0% without external memory, approaching the 72.1% achieved by Codex (GPT-5.5) with external memory. These results demonstrate post-training as an effective approach for improving robot planners. We will release the framework and trained planner checkpoints.</p></details> |  |
| **[Capable Teacher, Myopic Scorer: Reward-Free Teacher Repair for Agentic On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28586v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic on-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student agent on its own trajectories while a teacher scores every token. A natural explanation of where it goes wrong is compounding error: the student's mistakes carry its trajectories beyond the region where the teacher is reliable, so recent methods keep training inside that region, through episode-length curricula, teacher-guided turns, prefix replay, or teacher intervention where the two disagree. We show this explanation is incomplete. From the states where the student departs most from the teacher, the teacher's own takeovers still finish the task, yet the student trained on its token scores keeps running out of turns. We attribute this to the myopia of next-token scoring. The teacher rates each token without seeing where the episode ends, so its scores let through the safe, delaying moves that outcome-based RL would punish with zero reward, a penalty agentic OPD lacks. We call this difference between what the teacher does and what it scores the rollout-scorer gap. We propose Rollout-to-Scorer Distillation (R2S), which repairs the scorer before distillation by mining high-disagreement states from the untrained student, running teacher takeovers from them, keeping the states where enough takeovers pass a reward-free check such as consensus or an LLM judge, and fine-tuning the teacher to give its takeover-informed scores on every assistant token from the divergence turn onward, without the takeover in context. The repaired teacher is frozen as the sole scorer for standard OPD, requiring no task reward during repair or training. Across three environments, two teachers and four students, R2S outperforms three state-of-the-art agentic-OPD methods in all twelve teacher-student-environment cells, by 12.8 success points on average over the best of them in each cell. Project page: https://tree-shu-zhao.github.io/r2s/.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 5 figures, 13 tables. Project page: https://tree-shu-zhao.github.io/r2s/</p></details> |
| **[SceneRig: An Agentic System for Simulation-Ready 3D Scene Reconstruction from Single Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28585v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Simulation is a powerful tool for robotics, but building diverse, simulation-ready scenes at scale remains difficult. Reconstructing scenes from single images is a promising alternative, but visual similarity alone is insufficient: objects must also be recovered, accurately placed, and physically grounded. Existing methods that rely on post-hoc physics settling often misplace objects, producing scenes that look correct but behave poorly under manipulation. We present SceneRig, an agentic system that turns a single image into an editable, simulation-ready scene. SceneRig combines geometric, visual, and physical tools to reconstruct a faithful 3D scene. An agent then performs edit-and-validate loops inside a physics simulator to achieve physical stability. Across diverse inputs, SceneRig improves instance segmentation, object-centric appearance, and 3D placement while keeping the reconstructed scenes physically stable. Furthermore, SceneRig is effective for downstream robotics: its scenes predict real-robot policy outcomes more accurately than the baseline method and more reliably support replay of recorded manipulations without trajectory adaptation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at CoRL 2026 Agentic Robotics Workshop. Project page: https://red-fairy.github.io/SceneRig/</p></details> |
| **[Gibson Env: Real-World Perception for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/1808.10654v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Developing visual perception models for active agents and sensorimotor control are cumbersome to be done in the physical world, as existing algorithms are too slow to efficiently learn in real-time and robots are fragile and costly. This has given rise to learning-in-simulation which consequently casts a question on whether the results transfer to real-world. In this paper, we are concerned with the problem of developing real-world perception for active agents, propose Gibson Virtual Environment for this purpose, and showcase sample perceptual tasks learned therein. Gibson is based on virtualizing real spaces, rather than using artificially designed ones, and currently includes over 1400 floor spaces from 572 full buildings. The main characteristics of Gibson are: I. being from the real-world and reflecting its semantic complexity, II. having an internal synthesis mechanism, "Goggles", enabling deploying the trained models in real-world without needing further domain adaptation, III. embodiment of agents and making them subject to constraints of physics and space.</p></details> | <details><summary>Acces...</summary><p>Access the code, dataset, and project website at http://gibsonenv.stanford.edu/ . CVPR 2018 (spotlight)</p></details> |
| **[Cache Engineering for Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28577v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) agents can handle increasingly complex tasks, navigating long trajectories of observations and actions. As these trajectories grow, key-value (KV) cache I/O and storage become the dominant cost. Trajectories can also exceed the LLM's maximum context length, requiring control over active cache growth. Prior work uses context engineering to reduce cache size by trimming or summarizing history. However, modifying the trajectory prefix invalidates subsequent cached states and triggers costly re-prefilling of retained context. Task accuracy can also suffer when too little context is retained to limit re-prefill costs. We propose cache engineering as a new scheme. It allows agents to directly control their active KV cache without modifying the trajectory or re-prefilling retained context. Specifically, agents can proactively use drop to exclude selected cache from future attention and repos to adjust retained keys' position encodings. We formalize these cache operations and identify history dependence as a serving challenge: identical token sequences can yield different cache states under different cache operation sequences, making prefix matching insufficient for correct cache reuse. Thus, we co-design the AKV serving engine. It provides a stateless interface compatible with existing serving APIs, while internally managing stateful cache reuse, eviction, and restoration. Experiments show that a tool-response rolling-drop cache policy improves average benchmark scores by 1.7 points and reduces average cost by 27.7%, compared with conventional threshold-triggered summarization. With AKV, it achieves up to 1.2x the output throughput and a 1.5x speedup in P95 request latency. Recursive self-improvement of cache policies further reduces cost by 7.1% over expert design. Our code is at https://github.com/thu-nics/AKV.</p></details> |  |
| **[OverSeer: Multi-Agent World Models Beyond What Agents See](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28554v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite the success of video world models, extending them to multiple agents is difficult because it requires generating several agent-specific video streams that are consistent in time and between agents. To address this gap, we introduce OverSeer, a multi-agent world model that augments egocentric agent views with auxiliary camera views. We suggest that, by fixing the auxiliary views in space, they act as a proxy for a world-centric representation: Unlike the agents' views, which can change entirely due to a simple change in agent position, changes in the auxiliary views directly map to localized changes in the world state. We show that these fixed views substantially help the model to maintain consistency. We also show that, due to their redundant and semi-static nature, the auxiliary views can be efficiently compressed with a delta-VAE that encodes only the changes relative to a static reference image, reducing the number of tokens required to process them. Experiments in Minecraft and Doom demonstrate improved world and agent consistency, with qualitative results on real-world multi-camera captures indicating that the approach is not tied to game engines. Our compression analysis further shows that delta-VAE substantially reduces the computational overhead of auxiliary-view generation. We conclude that these fixed views provide a simple and efficient way to improve multi-agent world models.</p></details> |  |
| **[ASH: Agents that Self-Hone in Long-Horizon Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14211v6)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon visuomotor tasks remain a fundamental challenge in AI, as current methods rely on hand-engineered rewards or action-labeled demonstrations, neither of which scales. We introduce ASH, an agentic system that learns a long-horizon policy from unlabeled, noisy internet video, without reward shaping or expert annotation. ASH follows a self-improvement loop; when it gets stuck, ASH learns an Inverse Dynamics Model (IDM) from its own trajectories, and uses its IDM to extract supervision from relevant internet video. ASH uses unsupervised learning to identify key moments from large-scale internet video and retains them as long-term memory - allowing it to tackle long-horizon problems. We evaluate ASH on two complementary environments demanding multi-hour planning: Pokemon Emerald, a turn-based RPG, and The Legend of Zelda: The Minish Cap, a real-time action-adventure game. In both games, behavioral cloning, retrieval-augmented and zero-shot foundation-model baselines plateau, while ASH sustains progression across our 8-hour evaluation. ASH reaches an average of 11.2/12 milestones in Pokemon Emerald and 9.9/12 in Legend of Zelda, while the strongest baseline gets stuck in both environments at an average of 9.3/12 and 7.8/12 milestones, respectively. We demonstrate that self-improving agents are a scalable recipe for learning in long-horizon worlds.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[Proof-Carrying Contracts: Commitments AI Agents Cannot Break](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28484v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents increasingly act on behalf of people or other agents, purchasing services, exchanging information and delegating work. What an agent must respect is written in instructions, policies or agreements, but text alone cannot make it comply. It may make mistakes, be redirected by what it reads, or pursue different interests. For an economy of such agents to function, parties need agreements they can rely on without inspecting or trusting one another's agents. We introduce Proof-Carrying Contracts (PC2), a framework for making agreed constraints enforceable throughout an agent's interactions with a system. Before a requested operation can run, the agent must supply a machine-checkable proof that it preserves the agreed constraints in every case allowed by its model. An execution system called the Airlock checks the proof and controls access to the operation. The parties specify the conditions to preserve, while the agent supplies the reasoning needed to justify its decisions and remains free to adapt its strategy. This avoids having to design a separate permission policy and prove that every decision it permits is safe. We prove that the constraints hold at every instant during execution, even for an adversarial agent, and identify when this implies safety in the world. Reusable capability modules provide tools and guarantees from which contracts can be constructed. PC2 provides a foundation for an agent economy whose agreements protect both those delegating work and those carrying it out.</p></details> | 70 pages, 8 Figures, |
| **[For Coding Agents, the Repository Matters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.28235v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In July 2026, about 1,200 AI agents in an internal OpenAI evaluation, meant to run in isolation, used a shared package server as a message board, and about 700 of them joined an attack on Hugging Face. An automated scorer judged each agent on its own task, and the attack was coordinated through infrastructure the agents shared. Coding agents meet shared infrastructure every day, in the software repositories that receive their pull requests. Yet field studies still judge coding agents by outcomes pooled across repositories, such as merge rates and merge times. We asked how much these outcomes depend on the receiving repository, and whether they depend on it more than human contributions do. Using multilevel models, we compared agent pull requests from the AIDev dataset of public GitHub repositories with human pull requests in the same repositories. About two-fifths of the variation in how long agent pull requests take to be resolved lies between repositories, after accounting for the pull request, the agent, and the repository's observable attributes. In 280 repositories, with covariates common to both groups, the between-repository variance is 1.76 times as large for agent as for human pull requests (95% CI 1.41 to 2.18). For waits of several hours, a repository one standard deviation slower than average multiplies the typical wait by about 2.8 for agent and 2.2 for human pull requests. Rejection shows the same pattern, while for review rounds and comments the difference is not detectable. A proxy for the role of the developer who started the agent accounts for little of the difference, and stated agent policies were too rare to test. These results suggest that the oversight and evaluation of coding agents need the repository, and not only the agent, as a unit.</p></details> | <details><summary>11 pa...</summary><p>11 pages, 2 figures, 3 tables, followed by a 4-page supplement (Tables S1-S4, Figures S1-S2). v2: substantially revised and retitled; v1 was titled "Govern the Repository, Not the Agent: Measuring Ecosystem-Level Risk in AI-Native Software". Revised research questions, analysis and framing; replication package on Zenodo (doi:10.5281/zenodo.23504056)</p></details> |
| **[Toolflation: Seller Agents Distort Tool Markets Through Spontaneous Generative Engine Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28454v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agents are starting to sell to other agents. Seller agents write the listings of tools, services and products, and buyer agents read these listings when they choose what to use. We show that seller agents told only to be selected more often practice generative engine optimization on their own, and that this distorts the market for sellers and for buyers. We build tool markets from real data tools on Amazon and Instagram and run every tool on every question, so we know which tools can answer each question. Under all five production models we test, sellers rewrite their descriptions to claim more and more fields, including fields that their tools return for fewer than half of the targets. We call this process toolflation. Nothing in the market limits it, and how far it goes depends on the model, as the number of claims that contradict the sellers' own evidence differs by an order of magnitude between models. Toolflation shifts the buyers' choices between sellers, and the most capable tool loses share in seven of the ten combinations of seller model and platform. Under four models, the choices stay with capable tools and buyer agents keep their task success. Under the fifth, sellers copy one another until the descriptions no longer tell the tools apart. Buyer agents then pick tools by their position in the list, tools that answer almost nothing win a quarter to two fifths of the choices, and task success falls from 89% to about 30% on Amazon, close to random choice, and from 77% to 40% on Instagram. The sellers did only what their vendors asked, and the cost fell on buyers they never dealt with. We therefore argue that honesty toward third parties under competition is part of safety alignment, and that models should be tested as sellers before they are deployed as sellers.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 8 figures, 9 tables. Code: https://github.com/lilv98/Toolflation. Data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/lilvjosephtang/Toolflation</p></details> |
| **[After the Attacker Leaves: Benchmarking Carryover Attacks on LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28425v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern LLM agents persist state across sessions via two channels: long-lived memory files silently prepended to the system prompt, and scheduled tasks waking the agent without user input. A one-time indirect prompt injection committed to either channel can re-fire across sessions long after the attacker leaves, threats we term carryover attacks. Existing prompt-injection benchmarks evaluate attacks only in the session where malicious content is first observed, leaving the gate governing when and how often a planted payload later re-fires unmeasured. We present CarryoverBench: 12 personal-assistant scenarios and 101 attack tasks across three trigger channels: Instant, same-turn IPI; Autonomous, heartbeat-scheduled; and Latent, memory-resurfaced. A rule-based verifier scores planting and triggering as independent stages. Across five frontier models, planted payloads re-fire without measurable decay over five rounds; conditional on successful planting, the trigger stage often provides little additional protection. Porting the Latent channel to a second runtime, Hermes, attenuates but does not eliminate the effect, suggesting that carryover defense requires runtime-layer controls in addition to prompt-level interventions.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by EMNLP 2026 Main Conference</p></details> |
| **[A Multi-Agent Framework for Automated Machine Learning with Evidence-based Planning for Medical Image Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28410v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deep learning has become the default tool for medical image analysis, driven by the open-source movement. However, achieving the best performance on a given task remains difficult, with architecture selection and hyperparameter optimization (HPO) still considered "more of an art than a science". Automated machine learning (AutoML) automates parts of model training, yet helps little in medical imaging: general-purpose approaches lack medical architectures and preprocessing, and medical-focused ones demand engineering expertise and remain confined to narrow tasks such as 3D segmentation. We introduce MedicalMLOmni, a multi-agent AutoML framework that automates model training for diverse single- and multi-modal medical tasks through a natural language interface. Specifically, MedicalMLOmni performs evidence-based planning by retrieving published results to guide model selection and configuration, and employs parallel HPO where multiple agents explore adjustment strategies concurrently. MedicalMLOmni achieves the best score on 19 of 20 benchmarks; PMC-VQA is the exception, where a vision-language model with broader pre-training leads. Backbone-matched controls attribute the gains to the framework, not the backbone. By removing coding requirements, MedicalMLOmni lowers the barrier for domain experts with rich datasets but limited engineering expertise.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rewards for Budget-Aware Multi-Turn Agents That Adapt to Arbitrary Test-Time Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28409v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model agents are often given an inference budget and asked to maximise success within it. A multi-turn agent has to allocate this budget across many environment interactions. An external harness can enforce the budget, but it remains unclear whether the agent itself can learn when and how much to spend. We ask whether agents can learn this allocation across dynamically specified budgets and which rewards best teach this behaviour. We adapt existing length penalties to budget constraints spanning multiple resource types, introduce new reward formulations, and compare eleven objectives in a shared training pipeline. We train Qwen3.5 models at 4B and 9B on AppWorld with budgets over tokens, API calls, and environment steps, and on the BabyAI gridworld with turn budgets. We train a separate policy for each environment and resource type, with each policy trained across budget values. Training with rewards that penalise overspending substantially improves within-budget success and natural compliance over untrained models and over training on task success alone, with agents learning to adapt their resource use to stated budgets and task difficulty. These gains extend to unseen budgets and, zero-shot, to a shifted task distribution and a new benchmark. Reward objectives trade off success within budget and natural compliance. A sigmoid penalty for overspending most often achieves the highest within-budget success at an individual budget, while a fixed negative reward for overspending generally yields the strongest natural compliance. Combining penalties for overspend frequency and magnitude gives the smallest average gap to the best method at each budget. Overall, we show that a single agent can learn to allocate resources across turns and adapt its behaviour to user-specified budgets using budget feedback without external budget-allocation controllers.</p></details> | 32 pages, 8 figures |
| **[ORCA: Onboard Reflective Collaborative Agents for Reliable Task Offloading in LEO Satellite Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28404v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Low Earth orbit (LEO) satellites increasingly perform time-critical onboard processing, yet heterogeneous computing capabilities, rapidly changing inter-satellite links (ISLs) and uncertain neighbor behavior make decentralized task offloading difficult under partial observations. This paper presents ORCA, an architecture of Onboard Reflective Collaborative Agents for autonomous onboard task offloading. ORCA organizes offloading decisions into a four-Agent \emph{Perceive}$\rightarrow$\emph{Reason}$\rightarrow$ \emph{Act}$\rightarrow$\emph{Learn} loop. It aims to improve efficiency and reliability in dynamic, heterogeneous, and unreliable LEO satellite networks. The simulation results of a heterogeneous 60-satellite Walker-delta constellation demonstrates that ORCA delivers competitive system efficiency in cooperative settings while achieving superior task-success robustness and offloading reliability under previously unseen malicious node behavior, indicating that outcome-grounded reflection can improve the reliability of decentralized onboard offloading without centralized runtime coordination.</p></details> | <details><summary>6 pag...</summary><p>6 pages, 4 figures, IEEE Global Communications Conference (GLOBECOM) 2026 Workshop</p></details> |
| **[HexaShell: A Thin Harness for Exploring the Limits of VLM Agents as Robot Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23479v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agents built on vision language models (VLMs) now compete with learned policies on robot manipulation leaderboards. We ask how far a coding agent can go when it controls a robot instead of a computer, and where it stops. Our system, HexaShell, gives the agent a thin harness: one command line that turns its commands into robot motion and makes no decisions of its own. On the robot, the agent keeps doing what it does best: it reads output, writes code and runs tests. Two agents of the same model share the work. An acting agent runs each episode. After a failure, an optimizer agent reads the full record of the episode, with every command, image and message, finds the cause, and writes or fixes small programs called tools, each with its own tests. What the agents learn stays as code, and no demonstrations or training are needed. At run time, the tools use only what a real robot also has: RGB images, camera calibration and the state of the arms. They find objects by triangulation between camera views. Because these inputs are the same in simulation and on hardware, the tools move to a real robot with a different arm after only a small change for the arm. We build the tools on ten layouts of one seed per task with Codex and GPT-6-Astra and freeze them; the eight Open tasks share one set of tools. In RoboDojo's official evaluation on a hidden seed, 50 episodes for each of the 42 tasks, HexaShell reaches 46.9% success and a progress score of 53.2, the state of the art on the official RoboDojo leaderboard. The limit is contact, not reasoning: memory tasks reach 81.0%, but precision tasks only 32.0%. We open source the tools evolved so far at https://github.com/HexaFuture/HexaShell.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code:...</summary><p>Code: https://github.com/HexaFuture/HexaShell</p></details> |
| **[MAS-Guard: Unified Guardrails from Individual Agents to Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28397v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic AI extends large language models (LLMs) from generating responses to planning and executing complex workflows, substantially expanding their utility while exposing applications to unsafe actions, information leakage, and adversarial manipulation. Multi-agent systems further expand these capabilities through specialization and collaboration, but distributed authority, heterogeneous roles, and opaque or potentially compromised participants make safeguarding even more challenging. Existing defenses address important individual threats but provide fragmented coverage across local execution, system-level interactions, and enforcement mechanisms. We introduce MAS-Guard, a unified framework that closes this gap for safeguarding single- and multi-agent AI applications containing both instrumentable and opaque agents. Within instrumentable agents, the proposed local Agent Guardrail (A-Guard) layer applies task-specific experts to assess agentic events. At the system level, developer-owned Agent Charters and a Control Plane combine deterministic structural checks of routes, agent capabilities, and message formats with semantic assessments of roles and message content. These complementary layers enable enforcement through a shared set of interventions: allow, reflection, confirmation, and block. Evaluated across six benchmarks covering diverse single- and multi-agent scenarios with three agent LLMs from different providers, MAS-Guard reduces the overall attack success rate by 71.5-86.3% relative to unguarded agents, while meeting the utility reference criterion on benign tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[IDEAgent: Agentic Quality-Diversity Search for Research Idea Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22375v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have significantly advanced the automation of scientific discovery. However, existing systems share a core limitation: they optimize ideas independently for either Quality or Diversity. This often results in closely related ideas or large sets of trivial, unsound, or unclear concepts. We instead argue that research ideation should be framed as a conjunction of Quality and Diversity objectives, i.e., a Quality-Diversity (QD) search. Accordingly, we introduce IDEAgent, a multi-agent framework that tracks idea evolution through lineages. We jointly drive Quality using multi-objective feedback for repair and refinement, while Diversity is achieved through lightweight sequential memory and explicit comparison against completed ideas, historical variants, and rejected proposals. To evaluate this joint objective, we develop Yield, a metric that computes the largest mutually diverse set of ideas satisfying predefined quality thresholds. Across 32 topics spanning 8 CS domains, IDEAgent outperforms the best baseline by 3.89x on Yield, while achieving Yield of at least 2 on 8x as many topics. We further analyze quality improvements, showing that repair and refinement improve logical rigor and clarity while preserving non-obviousness. Lastly, we extend IDEAgent to two algorithm discovery tasks for verifiable quality judgment and find that it still consistently outperforms all baselines, and yields the largest set of high-quality and diverse programs that beat a trivial/standard baseline. To encourage future research on QD-search-based ideation, we open-source IDEAgent at https://github.com/declare-lab/IDEAgent.</p></details> | Under Review |
| **[AM-Bench: A Unified Taxonomy and Evaluation Suite for Agentic Misalignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24264v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As LLM-based agents are deployed with increasing autonomy in real-world environments, a critical failure mode emerges: agentic misalignment, where agents choose harmful actions over failure. However, existing safety benchmarks primarily evaluate whether agents comply with explicitly harmful instructions, leaving the conditions that cause agents to choose harmful actions poorly understood. To address this limitation, we propose AM-Bench, a unified taxonomy and 35 scenario evaluation benchmark spanning seven industries for agentic misalignment grounded in the fraud triangle, a well-established criminological framework explaining goal-directed misconduct. We adapt this framework to agentic environments and construct our evaluation suite and detection systems along three dimensions: perceived threat, perceived opportunity, and reasoning. We operationalize each dimension into structured subcategories rooted in prior theory: five threat categories derived from the instrumental convergence thesis, seven opportunity categories drawn from the Emergent Strategic Reasoning Risks (ESRR) taxonomy, and a novel four-question binary reasoning framework synthesized from prior literature. Our evaluation of 10 frontier models reveals a 62-percentage-point gap in misalignment across scenarios, with Gemini 3 Flash and Gemini 3.1 Pro exhibiting the highest levels of agentic misalignment while Claude Sonnet 4.6 displays near-zero rates across all conditions. Our reasoning framework further reveals that models may exhibit harmful behaviours without explicit situational awareness or explanation, complicating both mitigation and detection. AM-Bench scenarios and code are available at https://github.com/AI4Collaboration/Agentic-Misalignment-Bench</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted as a poster at NeurIPS 2026 Evaluations and Datasets Track</p></details> |
| **[Self-Credit: Credit Assignment for Long-Horizon Agentic RL via Intra-group Rescaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28388v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are commonly trained with RLVR, but the sparse nature of typical RLVR rewards makes credit assignment challenging, especially for long-horizon tasks. We propose Self-Credit, an end-to-end agentic RL method that creates dense supervision signals from sparse outcome rewards using self-generated credit assignment via intra-group rescaling. Self-Credit builds on group-based RL with three components. First, during its rollouts, the policy model generates summaries with citations to earlier turns containing critical evidence, which serve as self-generated credit. Second, we refine this model-generated credit with intra-group rescaling, which compares cited actions across successful and failed rollouts for the same prompt. This approach uses group-level agreement and disagreement to remove undeserved credit and further emphasize credit assigned to critical actions. Third, advantage reweighting uses the rescaled credit weights to update the advantages of the cited turns. On BrowseComp-Plus, Self-Credit improves over the base models by 37.0 and 36.2 points on Qwen3 8B and 32B respectively, and outperforms the strongest agentic RL baselines by 8.5 and 5.6 points, respectively. Self-Credit also improves over the strongest baselines by 4.9 points on GAIA and 4.7 points on xBench-DeepSearch. Analyses show that self-generated credit provides useful signals for selecting critical actions, and that intra-group rescaling, especially on failed rollouts, is important across model scales.</p></details> |  |
| **[CAPSafe: A Benchmark for Contextual Action and Plan Safety Reasoning in Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28381v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reasoning about safety is essential for embodied agents powered by large language models (LLMs). We introduce the Contextual Action and Plan Safety benchmark (CAPSafe), designed to evaluate safety reasoning at two levels: (1) an action-level dataset that tests whether a single action is safe given its environmental context, and (2) a plan-level dataset that evaluates whether agents can generate safe long-horizon plans for complex hazardous household tasks. CAPSafe models safety conditions where hazards are situational (inferred from object properties), multiple hazards can co-occur within the same environment, and each is annotated with a relative severity level for fine-grained analysis. Evaluating nine closed and open-source LLMs reveals a substantial gap between hazard recognition and safe long-horizon planning. Several models achieve high accuracy in recognizing unsafe actions (exceeding 90% in the best cases), yet safe long-horizon planning remains substantially harder. Gemini-3-pro achieves the highest safe success rate (78.19%), while other models remain below 65%. These results highlight that robust safety reasoning over long action sequences remains an open challenge.</p></details> |  |
| **[Object-Oriented Harness: Evolving Agent Harnesses through Object-Oriented Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28380v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent harnesses are executable code that specifies the prompts, tools, and control flow around a language model. A single global harness can struggle with heterogeneous tasks that require different execution strategies. In this work, we introduce Object-Oriented Harness (OO-Harness), a harness optimization framework that evolves a hierarchy of executable harness classes using object-oriented design. During training, an evolution agent uses execution records to create a family of specialized harnesses, each implemented as a subclass of the base or an existing specialized harness via inheritance. At test time, a harness router uses routing lessons accumulated during training to select a specific harness for each task. Across three long-horizon benchmarks spanning business workflows, workspace tasks, and tool use, OO-Harness achieves the best performance among the compared methods, with an absolute improvement of up to 15% over the base. | **[FALCA: Falsification-Aware Reinforcement Learning with Hierarchical Credit Assignment for Long-Horizon Deep Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28361v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon deep search requires LLM agents to iteratively decompose complex information needs, gather evidence from external sources, and maintain a reliable research state under multiple constraints. A central challenge is that early mistakes can be amplified over the search trajectory: agents may anchor on an incorrect candidate, collect partially supporting evidence, overlook contradictions, and accumulate errors that misguide subsequent reasoning. We propose FALCA, a falsification-aware framework that formulates deep search as a propose-falsify process. FALCA adopts a collaborative Searcher-Falsifier architecture, where the Searcher proposes candidate answers or sub-answers, while independent Falsifiers actively seek counterevidence, check unmet constraints, identify conflicts, and produce concise falsification reports for research-state revision. To strengthen this loop, we introduce falsification-aware training, combining SFT warmup on falsification trajectories with agentic RL based on parallel rollouts and hierarchical credit assignment over research stages, state transitions, and actions. Experiments on five search benchmarks show that FALCA improves long-horizon deep search performance over backbone and harness ablations, while reducing confirmation bias and error accumulation through more effective falsification and research-state revision.</p></details> | <details><summary>19 pa...</summary><p>19 pages, 20 figures, 2 tables. Project page: https://douzj-llm.github.io/FALCA/</p></details> |
| **[S-Agent 2.0: Spatial Reasoning with a General Coding Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28354v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatial reasoning asks a model to work out where objects are in 3D space, how they relate to one another, and how they move. Agentic VLMs have recently brought large improvements on such tasks by letting the model actively gather evidence with 3D perception tools instead of answering in a single pass. Even so, on many questions the tools return the right evidence and the model still does not arrive at the correct answer: the gap between model and tools, where raw readings become usable conclusions, is left unfilled. We present S-Agent 2.0, a spatial reasoning agent that equips an off-the-shelf general coding agent with a command-line perception toolkit, and we design the interface around one principle: help the VLM close the evidence loop: acquire scene evidence, remember it in a persistent scene memory, and interpret each reading in plain spatial language. This design keeps the full flexibility of a coding agent, and every reading it acts on stays grounded in the tools' geometry. On MMSI-Bench and ReVSI, the toolkit improves a strong open-source model, Qwen3.8-27B, by +13.9 and +20.5 points over the same agent without tools, and a strong closed-source model, Gemini-3.8-Flash, by +11.2 and +9.6 points. With the open backbone S-Agent 2.0 reaches 66.5 on MMSI-Bench and 82.3 on ReVSI, ahead of the specialized system SpatialClaw on the same backbone (62.6 and 74.5), and with Gemini-3.8-Flash it reaches 74.1 and 90.5 against its 67.0 and 79.7. Both tool gains shrink as the backbone grows more capable, but SpatialClaw's shrinks far more on ReVSI, from +13.2 to +2.7, while ours stays at +9.6. | **[Auto-Director: Leveraging Multi-Agent Collaboration for Autonomous Video Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28346v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous video editing from open-world resources remains a challenging problem in multimedia content creation. Retrieval-based methods are constrained by fixed source material and lack creative flexibility, while generative models struggle with long-horizon coherence and often diverge from user intent. To address these limitations, we present Auto-Director, a tool-augmented multi-agent framework that mirrors professional editorial workflows: a Planning Agent decomposes abstract topics into fact-grounded scripts via retrieval-augmented planning; a Material Agent indexes open-world footage through shot segmentation and visual tagging; an Editor Agent assembles semantically aligned clips with MLLM-driven spatial adaptation; and a Critic Agent governs an iterative Reflect-and-Revise loop that checks scene-level alignment and pacing. To rigorously evaluate this new paradigm, we introduce VEdit-Bench, a benchmark of 100 expert-curated topics with human-annotated Golden Scripts and a five-dimensional metric suite covering Narrative Logic, Factual Accuracy, Semantic Alignment, Compositional Quality, and Stylistic Cohesion. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Auto-Director significantly outperforms baseline methods in both automated metrics and human evaluation, effectively bridging the gap between amateurs and professional-grade video production.</p></details> | Accepted at MMM 2027 |
| **[Disclosure Begets Disclosure: A Self-Reinforcing Privacy Loop in LLM Agents with Persistent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28315v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use persistent memory to personalize responses, and personal attributes in this memory pose a privacy risk if exposed. In multi-session simulations, a simulated user with 24 personal attributes talks with a support agent that does or does not consult memory, and in both settings attributes are inferred from the full accumulated record. Across four support models, memory raises response quality but also empirical leakage, the share of attribute values correctly inferred from the record, by a margin that widens over sessions. The cause is a loop in ordinary, non-adversarial assistance. Memory-conditioned responses carry earlier values forward, often as presuppositions, and users affirm them or leave them uncorrected, so that cues and the agent's own guesses become recorded values. The loop reinforces itself, and removing the agent's mentions of an attribute lowers its later disclosure about twice as much as single-turn effects predict. Users cannot leave it on their own, since correcting a value discloses the attribute, declining to discuss it shifts later predictions toward sensitive values, and incorrect values left in place become entrenched. The loop is best weakened at the agent's response. Instructing the agent not to state or presuppose attributes brings leakage to about the no-memory level while keeping most of the quality gains, whereas filtering or anonymizing what users say or what is stored gives up much of them.</p></details> |  |
| **[GenoMAS: Guided Multi-Agent Programming for Scientific Discovery from Genomic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.21035v4)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Gene expression analysis holds the key to many biomedical discoveries, yet extracting insights from raw transcriptomic data remains challenging due to the complexity of multiple large, semi-structured files and the need for extensive domain expertise. Current approaches either rely on inflexible workflows that fail in edge cases or fully autonomous agents that lack the precision required for rigorous scientific inquiry. GenoMAS addresses this by presenting a team of LLM-based agents that integrates structured workflows with autonomous adaptability, orchestrating diverse specialized agents through typed message-passing protocols. At its core lies a guided-planning framework where programming agents decompose task guidelines into Action Units and dynamically choose to advance, revise, bypass, or backtrack, maintaining logical coherence while adapting to the idiosyncrasies of genomic data. On the GenoTEX benchmark, GenoMAS achieves 89.13% CSC for data preprocessing and 60.48% $F_1$ for identifying trait-associated genes, surpassing prior art by 10.61% and 16.85%. Beyond transcriptomics, it outperforms strong agent baselines on ScienceAgentBench and DiscoveryBench, two independent bioinformatics benchmarks. Qualitative analysis shows that GenoMAS identifies gene-phenotype associations that are biologically plausible and corroborated by the literature.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to EMNLP 2026 Findings. Project page at https://liu-hy.github.io/GenoMAS/ and code at https://github.com/Liu-Hy/GenoMAS</p></details> |
| **[MADDIE: Multi-Agent Drug Discovery Engine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16975v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Drug discovery remains a resource-intensive and time-consuming process, involving billions of dollars and a decade of development to advance a drug to market. While many in silico tools have accelerated this process, operating them still demands substantial time, effort, and expertise from medicinal chemists. Autonomous systems built on Large Language Models (LLMs) have emerged as a promising solution, yet current designs limit their performance. Existing single-agent systems scale poorly to long-horizon, multi-step, and realistic tasks, whereas multi-agent systems typically execute predefined workflows that cannot capture the iterative, non-linear nature of a real drug discovery campaign. Here, we present MADDIE, a multi-agent drug discovery engine. It comprises a central agent, Orchestrator, and four specialized worker agents: HitIdentifier, LeadOptimizer, Synthesizer, and Evaluator, each representing a critical step in a drug discovery campaign. Under Orchestrator's orchestration and coordination, these worker agents are composed into flexible workflows, enabling MADDIE to adapt its strategy and navigate the drug discovery pipeline end-to-end. Across 50 protein targets, MADDIE produces molecules satisfying key property requirements and their synthesis routes, outperforming existing agentic systems, generative models, and general-purpose LLMs by a wide margin. Our analysis reveals how these agents collaborate and that MADDIE can employ different strategies across targets to achieve the campaign's goal, while still being efficient in time and tokens. Together, they demonstrate MADDIE as a low-cost, effective, and efficient autonomous engine for accelerating drug discovery. Code and dataset is available via https://github.com/ninglab/MADDIE.</p></details> |  |
| **[Local Work, Global Progress: A Convergence Theory of AI Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28286v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Teams of large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly applied to complex tasks, but their collaboration protocols are mostly designed empirically, without convergence guarantees. We formalize collaborative problem solving as block optimization: each agent improves one part of a shared solution, guided by its local objective plus a correction for how its changes affect the other parts. A coordinator checks every proposal against the global objective and merges proposals that do not interfere with each other. We prove linear convergence up to an error floor that vanishes when the corrections are exact. Interfering proposals can also be merged once their combination has been checked; the rate then depends on how well the proposals actually combine, not on worst-case interactions. With GPT-OSS-120B agents on robot routing, a three-agent team finds collision-free plans in every run, better than those of a single agent that controls all robots. On a larger routing task, a six-agent team completes its search about twice as fast as the single agent (in median). Merging the best checked combination of proposals, rather than only proposals that do not interfere, cuts the team's search time by about 30%. With constants measured on the runs, our convergence bound holds in every round and follows the observed progress. We also test the theory on query planning in SQLite and on a synthetic task.</p></details> | <details><summary>59 pa...</summary><p>59 pages, 15 figures, 28 tables. Code: https://github.com/vectozavr/local-work-global-progress</p></details> |
| **[ChartEvo: Self-Evolving Agent Harnesses for Long-Tail Chart Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28282v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have substantially advanced chart understanding and reasoning, yet their performance remains highly uneven across chart types. While modern MLLMs perform well on common charts, their accuracy drops sharply on long-tail charts with diverse visual structures and reasoning requirements. Tool-augmented agents can improve complex chart reasoning by introducing specialized visual capabilities, but manually engineering strategies and tools for the heterogeneous and evolving long tail is difficult to scale. We introduce \textbf{ChartEvo}, a recursive self-improvement framework that enables chart agents to adapt to underperforming chart types through experience-driven harness-level self-improvement. ChartEvo jointly evolves reusable chart-specific Skills and executable Tools, while learning reusable local tool-call chains to compose evolved capabilities and control the inference overhead of tool-augmented reasoning. On long-tail charts from ChartBench and ChartX, ChartEvo improves accuracy over its base MLLM, Qwen3.7-Plus, by \textbf{45.6\%} and \textbf{32.5\%}, respectively, demonstrating the effectiveness of recursive self-improvement for long-tail chart adaptation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Adaptive-Group Process Rewards for GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17710v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training graphical user interface (GUI) agents with reinforcement learning suffers from sparse outcome rewards, leading to inefficient learning. Process rewards offer a promising direction for credit assignment to intermediate actions; however, accurately evaluating an action requires identifying evidence across long-horizon GUI trajectories, as its value may depend on information beyond its immediate state and observation. In this paper, we propose an adaptive-group process reward framework that dynamically partitions a GUI trajectory into semantically coherent subgoal groups, enabling process reward estimation at both the action and group levels. At the action level, a critic evaluates each action based on the visual changes and subsequent actions within the group for action correctness and contribution to the subgoal. At the group level, the framework aggregates action-level evaluations to assess subgoal completion and contribution to the overall task, and further refines these assessments by leveraging the context of all groups in the trajectory. The process reward of each action is obtained by combining its action-level evaluation with the refined group-level evaluation. We further augment GRPO's outcome advantage with the proposed process rewards to provide finer-grained guidance for GUI agent training. Experiments on process reward benchmarks and downstream RL training demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework.</p></details> |  |
| **[Agentic Communication Networks: When Agents Become Network Entities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28244v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous agents are moving from application-layer components to active participants in networked systems. They not only generate traffic but also perceive context, express intent, invoke services, coordinate with other agents, and act under task, policy, and resource constraints. This evolution exposes a mismatch between emerging agentic interactions and conventional endpoint- and traffic-centric network abstractions. This white paper introduces Agentic Communication Networks (ACN) as a network-centric paradigm for the time when agents become first-order network entities. At its core, ACN reshapes networking around three fundamental aspects. First, agents are represented as identifiable and governable network entities with explicit identity, authority, and lifecycle. Second, agentic services are expressed through intent, capability, context, policy, and task state, allowing the network to capture what an agent seeks to accomplish and under what conditions. Third, agentic execution becomes an explicit network concern as tasks involve multiple agents, services, tools, and distributed capabilities across heterogeneous infrastructures. Based on these shifts, the paper defines the scope, principles, architecture, and research directions of ACN, positioning it as a networking foundation for autonomous agent ecosystems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Subjective Valuations Make It Hard to be Fair: The Complexity of Resource Allocation to Groups of Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28213v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study the parameterized complexity of finding an EF1 allocation of indivisible items in the group-sharing setting, which has received increasing attention in recent years. There, items are not allocated to individual agents but rather to one of two (or possibly more) groups, and agents within that group all share the assigned items. We consider both the "fixed" (EF1-Fix) and "variable" (EF1-Var) group cases, where in the former the assignments of agents to groups are prescribed. While both are NP-hard in the classical sense, we show that their algorithmic behavior differs significantly from the parameterized perspective. As our main results, we: (1) prove that both cases are NP-hard even when the number of agents is a small constant; (2) circumvent this hardness by establishing fixed-parameter tractability for EF1-Var w.r.t. the number of "subjective" items, that is, items which are viewed as chores or goods by different agents---but rule out an analogous result for EF1-Fix; and (3) establish fixed-parameter tractability for EF1-Fix w.r.t. the number of item types, but link the existence of an analogous fixed-parameter algorithm for EF1-Var to a fundamental open question in the setting of Integer Linear Programming.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learn to Modify: Self-evolving Multi-agent Refinement Framework for Conversational Text-to-Motion Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28210v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite rapid progress in text-to-motion generation, existing methods are commonly designed for single-shot generation. When users are dissatisfied with specific motion details, they are often forced to regenerate the entire sequence from scratch, which is computationally inefficient and potentially destroys previously established kinematic context. Therefore, we introduce a conversational motion generation paradigm that requires the system to coordinate instruction understanding, motion generation, localized editing, and quality assessment. Recent advances in LLM-driven multi-agent systems provide a promising foundation for such role-specialized coordination. To tackle these challenges, a Self-evolving Multi-Agent RefinemenT Framework (SMART) is proposed for conversational motion generation. Within SMART, to preserve semantic consistency, we design a semantic-guided masked motion editing mechanism, which precisely grounds natural language feedback into spatiotemporal motion regions to enable localized refinements without altering unedited regions. To reduce redundant exploration, we propose a memory-based self-evolving strategy that dynamically accumulates motion editing knowledge from interaction history and reuses past successful experience. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SMART effectively supports multi-turn conversational motion generation and enables efficient motion refinement.</p></details> |  |
| **[MaD-RSI: Model-as-Data for Recursive Self-Improvement of Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28201v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recursive self-improvement (RSI) of agent harnesses has emerged as an effective approach to enhancing the capabilities of large language model (LLM) agents. By searching the harness space for a specific LLM on a given dataset, RSI can discover a harness tailored to the capabilities and behavioral characteristics of that model. However, such optimization often creates a strong coupling between the learned harness and its target LLM, limiting generalization across models and requiring substantial computational resources to adapt to new LLMs. Moreover, existing RSI methods typically optimize a harness using feedback from a single model and evaluate its generalization to other models only after training. This paradigm treats model differences primarily as a criterion for evaluating generalization rather than as valuable evidence for optimization. In this paper, we introduce a new perspective that treats models as data and places the harness at the center of optimization. Building on this perspective, we propose \textsc{MaD-RSI}, a framework that directly optimizes a shared harness across multiple heterogeneous LLMs by incorporating their diverse failure trajectories into the training process. Rather than assessing model differences only after optimization, \textsc{MaD-RSI} leverages these differences as training evidence to guide the self-improvement of a shared harness. Furthermore, we introduce a new evaluation framework with two complementary metrics designed to assess the effectiveness of harness optimization across models. Experiments across multiple datasets and LLMs demonstrate that \textsc{MaD-RSI} improves generalization over existing approaches while reducing training costs.</p></details> |  |
| **[ROAR: Benchmarking LMM Agents under Real-Time Constraints in Multiplayer Action-Shooter Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24282v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large multimodal models (LMMs) are increasingly deployed as agents that perceive, reason, and act in interactive multi-agent environments. Yet most existing benchmarks for LMM-based agents in multi-agent settings remain turn-based: the environment and other agents pause while an agent performs inference. Such protocols obscure a fundamental challenge of real-time constraints, where the world continues to evolve and delayed decisions may become outdated before execution. Motivated by this limitation, we introduce ROAR, a multiplayer action-shooter game environment for evaluating LMM-based agents in multi-agent settings under real-time constraints. ROAR covers cooperative, competitive, mixed cooperative-competitive, and basic single-agent scenarios. We further propose a Multi-Agent Asynchronous Gym interface that decouples environment evolution from agent reasoning by running the environment and each agent in separate threads, enabling the environment to advance at fixed time intervals without waiting for LMM inference and allowing agents to reason independently without blocking one another. ROAR also features convenient deployment, multimodal observations, and customizable levels. ROAR offers a practical testbed for advancing LMM-based agents beyond static, turn-based evaluation toward real-time multi-agent decision-making.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[AgenTest: Mining Business Rules to Test LLM Agents for Business Logic Bugs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28116v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly execute business operations through tools and interact directly with application data and services. Beyond completing user requests, these agents must obey domain-specific business rules that constrain when actions are permitted and what data or resources may be affected. Violating such rules can lead to business logic bugs, even when a task appears successful. Detecting these bugs is difficult because the rules are rarely available as explicit, testable specifications. They are distributed across system prompts and tool definitions, while others remain implicit in domain knowledge. Existing agent testing approaches typically evaluate task success, predefined policies, or known attack scenarios. This limits their ability to systematically uncover previously unspecified business-rule violations. We present AgenTest, which mines business rules from system prompts, tool definitions, and domain knowledge, refines them into Given-When-Then specifications, and systematically tests each specification. For each extracted specification, AgenTest instantiates its Given conditions by constructing a satisfying environment state and simulated user, exercises the agent through multi-turn interactions, and checks its Then conditions over messages, tool calls, and state changes using a hybrid oracle. On the airline, retail, and telecom agents of tau2-bench with two underlying models, AgenTest produces 551 refined specifications, and detects violations of 89 and 121 distinct specifications, respectively -- 3.4X and 2.5X as many as all baselines combined. Only AgenTest detects cases where an agent improperly accesses or modifies another user's data. Moreover, about 60% of bug-triggering runs cannot be detected from the final state alone, highlighting the need to check the full interaction against explicit business rules.</p></details> |  |
| **[FIT-MAC: A Closed-Loop Multi-Agent Framework for Robust Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28109v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language-action (VLA) models have achieved strong performance on standard robotic manipulation benchmarks. However, their reliability can degrade substantially when task conditions deviate from the nominal setting. Perturbations in object positions and task instructions may lead to complete task failure. Existing VLA policies generally lack an explicit runtime mechanism for distinguishing these failure modes and selecting an appropriate recovery strategy. To systematically mitigate this issue, we propose FIT-MAC, a failure-aware, closed-loop multi-agent control framework for improving the robustness of VLA policies at inference time. FIT-MAC augments a VLA policy with an inference-time control layer. First, the Planner Agent parses the language instruction into a structured task specification that captures the manipulation goal and spatial constraints, and the Grounder Agent resolves each instruction-level reference to a visual target. Next, the Verifier Agent tracks phase-specific execution progress, distinguishes target-binding errors from local execution failures, and determines whether intervention is warranted. The Correction Agent then routes permitted interventions to target rebinding or bounded grasping or placement recovery, depending on the failure type. When no intervention is required, the pretrained VLA remains the default action generator. Extensive experiments on LIBERO-PRO demonstrate that FIT-MAC improves robustness across multiple VLA backbones, consistently increasing success rates and achieving state-of-the-art performance under diverse perturbations.</p></details> |  |
| **[ClawMMU: Protection-Coherent Virtual Memory for Stateful LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28093v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents keep long-term memory, much of which comes from web pages and tool results. Agent harnesses, the software that runs the model, do not record who may act on this memory, so an instruction hidden in a fetched page can be treated as the user's own instruction. We propose ClawMMU, a protection layer that labels every piece of memory with its owner, scope, origins, and authority. ClawMMU checks these labels whenever content enters the model's context, is written to storage, returns from storage, or drives a tool call. Its central rule is that information may rise in authority, but authority never lifts itself. Because the checks depend on where content came from, not on what it says, memory attacks become classical protection faults that the harness stops even when the model is fooled.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the AgenticOS Workshop @ NeurIPS 2026 (non-archival). 6 pages, 1 figure, 1 table</p></details> |
| **[HSPR: Recovering Software-Agent Benchmark Scores via Hierarchical Posterior Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24611v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluating software agents on a full benchmark is expensive. A small task subset can preserve agent rankings while yielding inaccurate scores. Historical responses provide useful structure, but tasks with identical source responses can have different outcomes for a new target agent. We introduce Hierarchical Score Posterior Recovery (HSPR), which combines source-fitted response factors with uncertain group success rates. Observed target outcomes update the posteriors over the target latent state and group rates; posterior-guided acquisition selects subsequent tasks. Its score-based criterion equals the expected one-step reduction in full-score posterior variance. Observed successes and posterior-predictive counts for unqueried tasks yield a full-score posterior. The final estimate uses its median with source-selected calibration. At a 10% task budget on four task banks from the SWE-bench family, HSPR achieves the lowest mean absolute error (MAE) on three banks and the lowest maximum absolute error (MaxErr) on all four among the methods compared. Removing group uncertainty or replacing adaptive queries increases macro MAE in the respective controls. Macro MAE decreases across 5-20% budgets. | **[Training the Whole Agent, Not Just the Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28069v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training coding agents typically updates the policy while keeping the harness fixed. | **[InteractAgent: Agentic Human Motion Interaction with Memory-Augmented Reflection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25649v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon human-scene interaction requires coordinated spatial reasoning, physical feasibility, and the ability to recover from compounding execution errors. InteractAgent integrates Online Observation for step-wise recovery decisions (continue/redo/replan) and Memory-Augmented Reflection for multi-round plan refinement with adaptive stopping. | **[HydraPoT: A Configurable Multi-Agent Framework for Cost-Aware LLM-Assisted Honeypots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27722v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) can improve honeypot realism by generating dynamic, context-aware responses, but powerful cloud models incur substantial cost and latency, while lightweight models may provide weaker deception. We evaluate HydraPoT through an architectural-overhead analysis, a cross-agent context-preservation benchmark, and a three-agent case study. HydraPoT introduces less than 5% runtime overhead, preserves context across heterogeneous agents, and achieves fidelity close to a cloud-LLM-only honeypot. | **[When Does Experience Memory Help Closed-Loop LLM Driving Agents? A Controlled Evaluation with Resampled Warmup Pools, Token-Matched Fillers, and Audited Baselines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27714v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When does experience memory help a closed-loop LLM driving agent? Published gains cannot say, because the two factors we find to matter most, which warmup experiences the pool contains and what any injected tokens do to the prompt, are controlled in none of them. We answer with a controlled evaluation on one audited HighwayEnv testbed (1Hz, gpt-4o-mini for the decisive contrasts). We treat the warmup pool as a resampled random factor (ten disjoint pools) and add token-matched irrelevant-filler controls, oracle-certified diagnostics, caution-pricing metrics, and quantified audits of the baseline ports. The probe suite spans flat-text retrieval (a DiLu port), a single-frame grid port (RESPOND-inspired), our episodic temporal-graph memory, a retrieval-free consolidated rulebook, and a flat K-step observation-history baseline. Four findings follow, each a property of this testbed. (1) Headline memory gains belong to the warmup draw. The reported pool is the best of ten for both retrieval memories, which beat No-Memory in only one pool of ten (sign test p=0.011), and even the rulebook's apparent pool-immunity collapsed on five fresh draws. (2) No retrieval design tested exceeds token-matched filler. The collision-only merge ceiling is reached by a single 15-token caution instruction, and metrics that price caution erase the rulebook's lead. (3) Representational sophistication is nowhere robustly operative. (4) Baseline port quality silently inverts conclusions. On this testbed, a single-pool comparison without filler controls would have reported memory gains that these controls reverse. We release the testbed, audited ports, seeds, and caches.</p></details> | ACML 2026 |
| **[SAFE-Mem: Secure Agentic Memory with Fine-Grained Authority and Governance-Aware Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22959v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on persistent memory to support long-term reasoning and personalized interactions. To address these limitations, we propose SAFE-Mem, a fine-grained memory governance framework that improves security while preserving legitimate memory use. First, SAFE-Mem annotates each claim with three governance labels (i.e., kind, authority, and scope) to capture its provenance and permitted use. Second, governance-aware retrieval matches query requirements with claim permissions to block unauthorized influence while retaining legitimate information. Third, a governance-enabled memory graph connects source sentences, claims, and entities to support provenance tracking and efficient retrieval. Across four LLM backbones on MPBench and LoCoMo, SAFE-Mem attains the lowest attack success among defenses that retain legitimate memory, at least 77% below the strongest of them, and the highest answer accuracy of all evaluated defenses, improving it by up to 15 points. | **[OpenAgenet / OAN White Paper: Open Infrastructure for Trusted Agent Interconnection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03161v6)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>OpenAgenet, abbreviated as OAN, is an open infrastructure project for trusted Agent interconnection. OAN is designed as a protocol-neutral trust layer. It does not replace Agent interaction protocols, tool protocols, model orchestration frameworks, or application-level workflows. Instead, it provides routing-aware did:oan-based resource identity, local OAN Identity material for signing and authentication, governance-backed admission, Registrar-assisted onboarding, Root-verified package publication, authorization-aware Discovery, Root-issued infrastructure authorization VCs, controller-aware authorization, and profile-defined signed trusted invocation. The architectural center of OAN is the combination of federated governance, resource identity, trusted Discovery, and the Root Node's three hub responsibilities. Root acts as a trust authorization hub for accepted identity state and evidence, a data-distribution hub for Root-verified packages, and a semantic-governance hub for the tag tree shared by registration and Discovery. This white paper explains the motivation, architecture, roles, governance model, relationship with MCP, A2A, and ANP, deployment patterns, cooperation model, on-chain governance layer, prototype status, public pilot network, standardization surfaces, performance profile, and roadmap of OAN.</p></details> |  |

| **[Is AI Widening the Wage Gap? The results show that education subsidies targeted at low-income workers increase both the number of skill-upgrading attempts and the number of successful upgrades, with particularly pronounced improvements in the upskilling success probability of workers in the bottom income decile. | **[From Debate to Decision: Conformal Social Choice for Safe Multi-Agent Deliberation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.07667v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent debate improves LLM reasoning, yet agreement is not evidence of correctness.
