Verbalized probability distributions from heterogeneous agents are aggregated via a linear opinion pool and calibrated with split conformal prediction, yielding fixed-round marginal coverage: at any pre-specified round, the correct answer is included with probability ${\geq}\,1{-}α$, without assumptions on individual model calibration. A hierarchical action policy maps singletons to autonomous action and larger sets to human escalation. Across eight MMLU-Pro domains and two additional benchmarks, fixed-round coverage stays close to target. The key finding is not that debate becomes more accurate, but that the conformal layer makes its failures actionable: at the final round, 81.9\% of wrong-consensus cases are flagged by non-singleton sets at $α{=}0.05$. Separately, among cases that reach a singleton before the final round, accuracy reaches 90.0--96.8\% on MMLU-Pro (up to 22.1pp above consensus stopping): a selection effect, not a reasoning improvement. Simple probability- and entropy-threshold rules attain comparable interception; conformal's distinct value is a finite-sample fixed-round coverage guarantee together with explicit candidate sets for escalation. Under a fixed simulated user no cell shows a MAS benefit: the MAS is worse in 12 of the 13 valid cells and tied in the thirteenth, and the fitted gain is negative across the observed capability range (weighted mean -28 points, 95% CI [-44, -12]). CUA-Recovery comprises a human-verified test suite of 2,250 erroneous states over 100 tasks and a training suite of 8.6K trajectories over 377 tasks. Evaluation on the test suite shows that agents struggle both to recognize inherited errors and to complete the task. Across six agents and six takeover depths, Error Awareness Rate (EAR) reaches at most 44.0% and Pass@3 at most 25.5%. | **[From Outcomes to Steps: Reliable Success-Aware Critic Learning for Long-Horizon Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27623v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning for long-horizon language agents requires identifying whether intermediate actions make eventual task success more or less likely when supervision is available only at trajectory level. A compositional authorization failure (CAF) is a locally authorized execution, or a pair of locally authorized executions, that violates a global policy spanning intent, purpose, provenance, consent, and delegation. We characterize CAFs along two axes, expressiveness (single-trace safety versus relational k-safety) and observability (a ladder of monitor profiles). Our central result is an impossibility: relational purpose violations form a 2-safety hyperproperty with no per-trace locus of blame, so a per-action monitor that never raises a false alarm detects none of them, independent of the underlying model or decision procedure; they become visible only under cross-execution monitoring, which none of the six guardrails we survey implements. We make this constructive with AL-SYNTH, a SAT/SMT synthesizer (using no language model) that emits minimal CAF witnesses, each checked against an exhaustive oracle. The empirical signature is sharp: across nine LLM judges the per-action view approves the synthesized witness almost without exception, blocking it at a rate comparable to a benign control, yet the same models flag it when shown the two executions together. Milton stood 11th of 460 agents in bargaining at the fixed stop; we also report the close, where the final day's pool shift moved all five agents' bargaining ratings together while their play was unchanged, and three dispatch defects were found by checking delivered behavior against the design.</p></details> |  |
| **[HABIT-Bench: Evaluating Habit Induction from Longitudinal Weak Evidence in Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24520v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In long-term interactions with large language model (LLM) agents, users reveal habits through their choices and actions. We introduce HABIT-Bench to evaluate how well agent memory systems infer habits from weak evidence and apply them to later requests. We construct synthetic user histories from task-oriented dialogue data and controlled habit graphs. The histories span hundreds of sessions across food, finance, software, and travel. Four-choice tasks assess agents' ability to infer user habits and apply them appropriately. They include temporary exceptions, changes in habits, and suggestions that users have not accepted. Sampled tasks undergo independent human review. We evaluate eight memory methods with Qwen3-235B-A22B and GLM-5.3-Flash. The best-performing memory method achieves 33.61\% macro accuracy across the four domains. Performance remains limited even when annotated evidence is supplied directly. These results expose a persistent gap between access to past interactions and the ability to infer and appropriately use user habits.</p></details> |  |
| **[Clarify or Answer: Reinforcement Learning for Agentic VQA with Context Under-specification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.16400v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Real-world visual question answering (VQA) may be context-dependent: an image-question pair could be under-specified, such that the correct answer depends on external information that is not observable in the image. In such cases, directly answering can lead to confident but incorrect predictions. CoA first determines whether clarification is necessary; if so, it generates one or more clarification questions to iteratively enrich the context until ambiguity is resolved. To validate this framework, we introduce CONTEXTCLARIFY, a dataset of ambiguous VQA questions along with a contrast set of non-ambiguous questions. We further introduce GRPO-CR(Clarification Reasoning), a reinforcement learning approach that optimizes clarification question generation with multiple reward signals encouraging well-formed, focused, non-trivial questions that resolve ambiguity. Across three VLMs and three datasets, CoA achieves consistent improvements at both the module and system levels, with a single clarification turn providing substantial gains and recursive multi-turn execution offering modest additional improvements in select settings.</p></details> |  |
| **[Secure Agentic Coding through Counterexample-Grounded Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25916v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As coding models and agent harnesses become more capable, reliable security verification is becoming a central bottleneck. Existing model-based approaches require models to either infer program behavior from source code or specify correct outcomes for adversarial tests, placing substantial reasoning demands on the security signal. We introduce \falcon{}, a counterexample-grounded verification framework that separates attack exploration from behavioral verification and connects them through execution traces of the program under test. In test-time repair, \falcon{} yields substantially stronger held-out functional-security gains than alternative security signals across secure code generation benchmarks, while preserving or improving functionality. As an RL reward, \falcon{} also outperforms static-analysis and learned rewards on held-out functional-security, even when all policies successfully optimize their respective training rewards. Combining \falcon{} with either proxy reward further improves held-out performance, suggesting that execution-grounded feedback can mitigate reward overoptimization while preserving useful guidance from existing security signals.</p></details> |  |
| **[Act on Your Behalf, Beyond Your Authorization: Benchmarking LLM Agent Authorization Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27548v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When an LLM agent acts on behalf of a user, it is expected to act within the authorization scope the user grants. A scalable construction pipeline produces 286 executable tasks spanning situational triggers, the presence or absence of direct harmful consequences, and ten application domains. We execute every task with four models at two reasoning-effort settings across three agent harnesses in an authorization-aware sandbox that keeps authorized and out-of-authorization paths executable and observable, yielding 6,864 trajectories. Agents act out of authorization in 19.2% of trajectories, with rates ranging from 10.5% to 26.6% across the 24 configurations. Task completion does not imply authorization. Increasing reasoning effort leaves the aggregate rate nearly unchanged, with effects varying across models. On controlled pairs, agents act out of authorization at similar rates with (17.9%) and without (20.8%) direct harmful consequences, and more often under benign pressures than malicious injections (23.5% versus 17.6%, p=0.002). Our results show that task capability, additional reasoning, and consequence-oriented safety evaluation do not by themselves ensure authorization alignment. Moreover, agents that consult the principal before completing a task exhibit lower rates of out-of-authorization behavior (14.0% versus 21.6%, p<0.001), pointing to a tension between agent autonomy and preserving users' decision authority that calls for further human-centered study. | **[SpatialScout: Can VLM Agents Actively Explore and Map 3D Scenes?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26659v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatial intelligence requires agents to actively acquire visual evidence and integrate observations into a coherent representation of the three-dimensional world. We introduce SpatialScout, a benchmark for evaluating vision-language model (VLM) agents on active object-level 3D mapping. SpatialScout comprises 80 diverse indoor scenes containing 3,140 target objects. Given an object inventory, RGB observations, camera calibration, and camera poses, agents control a camera to explore each scene and estimate object centers in a shared coordinate frame. The task requires coordinating viewpoint selection, cross-view association, geometric estimation, and map revision under bounded interaction budgets. Evaluation jointly measures localization accuracy and target coverage through object-scale overlap scores that penalize missing predictions. Experiments with frontier VLMs reveal substantial limitations: the strongest evaluated model, Claude Opus 5.5, achieves a mean overlap score of 41.71%, with 47.23% of targets exceeding an intersection-over-union (IoU) threshold of 0.5 and only 3.25% exceeding 0.9. These results reveal a substantial gap between recovering coarse object layouts and constructing complete, precise metric maps. SpatialScout provides a quantitative testbed for developing agents that build reliable 3D spatial representations through active perception. | **[Automated Multi-Agent Organization Design: From Architecture Search to Organizational Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27531v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic system design is increasingly shifting from hand-crafted prompts and workflows toward automated search over agentic programs and interaction structures, with multi-agent systems receiving growing attention. Yet a central question remains unresolved: when does an automatically generated multi-agent structure exhibit a functionally meaningful division of responsibility? We argue that automated multi-agent design should move beyond searching for effective architectures toward identifying which responsibilities are functionally necessary, how they should be allocated, and when they should change. To explore this, we propose Automated Multi-Agent Organization Design (AMOD), which treats organization itself as a first-class design object, explicitly modeling how agent teams are composed, what functional responsibilities the agents assume, how they coordinate, and how collective decisions are made. We formulate AMOD as an execution-driven design loop. For each task, an automated designer instantiates an organization by assigning functional responsibilities and coordination rules to a set of agents. After execution, task outcomes, intermediate artifacts, and interaction traces are examined to identify which responsibilities may be missing, duplicated, or ineffective, and the organization is revised accordingly. We also propose testing discovered responsibilities by removing them, replacing them with generic roles, or changing their assignment under matched resource budgets, with complementary evaluations. | **[Repair the Core, Not the Symptom: Planning-Graph Repair for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01767v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon language agents make interdependent decisions across multiple rounds, forming growing planning graphs in which later decisions depend on earlier states, observations, and tool outputs. Errors in these graphs can therefore propagate through subsequent decisions and amplify over long rollouts. Correcting the most visible failure may therefore fix a symptom while leaving the core that drives downstream errors unchanged. Replaying the entire planning graph provides the opposite extreme, but becomes increasingly expensive as the graph grows and forces an LLM to reason over substantial irrelevant context. We study planning-graph repair: given a failed rollout, the goal is to identify and correct the compact core responsible for continued error propagation while preserving the remainder of the graph. We first evaluate a range of practical engineering repair strategies, including single-error and TopK repair, temporal windows, neighborhood expansion, cascade-aware repair, and full-graph LLM correction. We then propose ReCore (Repair the Core), which models the coupled propagation of node and edge errors to identify the core of a failed planning graph. Rather than repairing the largest observed errors or expanding a fixed local neighborhood, ReCore selects a connected core whose correction maximally reduces residual error amplification, and exposes only this core to the LLM for diagnosis and repair. We also theoretically establish a bound on long-horizon planning regret. Extensive experiments on synthetic Agent Calling-Tree graphs and the Who\&When attribution benchmark show that ReCore improves long-horizon stabilization and root-cause localization by targeting high-amplification repair regions, matching full-context recovery with up to $7.4\times$ fewer tokens for every successful repair.</p></details> | Under Review |
| **[Ask the World Before Acting: Environment Probing for Calibrating Agent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31422v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon language agents enable autonomous decision-making in complex environments by planning and executing sequences of interdependent actions. To act reliably, these agents must maintain an accurate world model that tracks the environment's evolving state and supports subsequent decisions. However, as interactions accumulate, stored beliefs can become outdated or incorrect, causing agents to plan and act on assumptions that no longer reflect the environment. Such errors are difficult to resolve through additional reasoning or self-reflection alone, because the information needed for correction must be obtained from the environment. Actively probing the environment therefore offers a direct way to verify and correct these beliefs. To this end, we introduce ENVPROBE, a framework that enables agents to actively explore their environments and correct inaccurate beliefs during long-horizon execution. Specifically, ENVPROBE prioritizes belief fields for verification based on task relevance and potential errors. It then selectively probes the environment to retrieve the actual value of a chosen field and incorporates the observation into the agent's world model. With the updated beliefs, the agent revises its plan and continues execution based on the newly acquired environmental information. Extensive experiments across three controlled environments demonstrate that ENVPROBE substantially improves terminal world-state accuracy compared with agents operating without environment probing, leading to more reliable long-horizon decision-making. These results demonstrate the importance of actively verifying and correcting beliefs through environmental interactions for maintaining accurate world models in long-horizon language agents.</p></details> | Under Review |
| **[Agentic vs. Parametric World Models: Hybrid Planning for Reliable Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.27806v4)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language agents, such as ReAct, Reflexion, and RAP, implicitly predict how the world will change as they plan and act, using imagined state transitions to guide their decisions. These predictions are encoded in the agents' backbone LLMs as internal beliefs about the environment, effectively forming an implicit world model for multi-step planning. However, hallucinated state information can become embedded in this world model, leading to persistent inaccuracies that influence subsequent decisions. Traditional parametric world models, such as Dreamer and MuZero, learn environment dynamics through explicit transition models, but their limited model capacity makes them difficult to apply directly to modern language-agent planning. We study this tradeoff in graph-structured planning environments and seek to combine the strengths of both paradigms. We propose Hybrid World-Model Planning (HYBRID-WM), which preserves the language agent's semantic planning capabilities while leveraging a lightweight parametric world model for more accurate transition predictions. Specifically, the parametric model constructs a compact planning skeleton to guide action generation while maintaining consistency between the agent's imagined transitions and the model's predictions. Experiments across four graph-structured planning benchmarks demonstrate that HYBRID-WM consistently improves task success and reduces hallucinated-state propagation with modest computational overhead. These findings highlight the effectiveness of combining semantic planning with lightweight parametric grounding for reliable long-horizon language-agent planning.</p></details> | Under Review |
| **[MemDev-Bench: Does Memory Help Code Agents?Benchmarking Memory Systems over Real Repository Histories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27524v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Code agents increasingly work on evolving real repositories, and memory systems aim to help them reuse earlier experience. However, such experience is diverse and evolving, so it remains unclear how memory systems organize it and thereby affect code agents. Existing benchmarks cannot answer this reliably because they lack either temporally valid histories or control over prior-task relevance. In this paper, we present MemDev-Bench to evaluate how memory systems affect code agents over diverse repository histories. It pairs each task with relevance-annotated earlier tasks from the same repository to build histories with controlled relevance. We also build an automated pipeline that reliably makes these earlier tasks executable and verifiable, producing 4,869 historical tasks for 555 base tasks. Based on MemDev-Bench, our systematic evaluation of memory systems across diverse development scenarios yields three main findings. (1) Memory mainly improves fine-grained localization and editing, increasing functional correctness. (2) Memory also causes failures on otherwise solvable tasks even with related history, and unrelated history tends to increase such failures. These failures mainly stem from agents reusing memory without reassessing its applicability. (3) Compared with using memory systems, directly providing agents with raw historical trajectories substantially reduces their steps. Further analysis suggests that agents save these steps by reusing process information that compressed memories tend to omit. These results suggest that future memory systems should preserve process information and reassess memory applicability against current task context.</p></details> |  |

## Drug Design
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Distillation, Self-Training, and the Fragility of Learned Chemical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24199v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automating synthesis planning for small-molecule drug design requires chemical reasoners that are accurate as well as open to inspection and adaptation, yet frontier language models remain unreliable at predicting reaction outcomes and difficult to deploy, while small open-weight models lack the necessary domain reasoning. We show that frontier supervision can serve as a one-time bootstrap for open models on forward reaction prediction. Our models, Resonance, distill an open-weight teacher's reasoning traces and then learn from their own verified outputs, turning a small base model with near-zero accuracy into a capable reaction predictor, while a stronger student surpasses its teacher through distillation alone. Accuracy, however, does not bring robust reasoning: the small student inherits its teacher's single-pass style, self-training mainly sharpens answers it could already reach, and it readily abandons correct answers under pushback, suggesting that chemical reasoning models should be evaluated on the robustness of their reasoning as well as on the accuracy of their answers.</p></details> | 10 pages, 7 figures |
| **[On the Selectivity of Generative Models in Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24124v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Designing small-molecule drugs that selectively bind to a target protein while avoiding unintended interactions with other proteins is essential for reducing side effects that contribute substantially to clinical attrition. Although structure-based drug design (SBDD) has advanced rapidly with deep learning, most generative models still optimize binding to a single target and disregard selectivity due to limited training data and unknown off-target interactions. Motivated by this gap, we introduce SELECTBENCH, a comprehensive benchmark assessing the selectivity of generative SBDD models. SELECTBENCH provides three curated evaluation tasks reflecting different real-world drug discovery scenarios: literature-based case studies, safety screening panels used in pharmaceutical development, and large-scale structure-based evaluation sets. We further introduce a suite of metrics to quantify selectivity and conduct a systematic evaluation of nine representative SBDD models, including methods explicitly trained for selectivity and widely-used inference-time guidance techniques. Our results show that even selectivity-trained models achieve only modest off-target discrimination and inference-time guidance provides limited gains, underscoring the need for method improvement. SELECTBENCH provides both a characterization of where current generative SBDD stands on selectivity and an open, extensible platform to accelerate progress. The benchmark is available at https://github.com/lovetaro/selectbench.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted as a Presentation at the Neural Information Processing Systems 2026</p></details> |
| **[DrugHarness: An Evolvable Agent Harness for Computational Drug Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23791v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Computational drug discovery requires agents to compose scientific operations and adapt to changing evidence and constraints. Here we introduce DrugHarness, an evolvable agent harness coupling composable scientific execution with conditional skill induction. Its environment integrates over 80 scientific operations, biomedical resources and task-specific code, preserving associations among candidates, results and artifacts. From execution trajectories and outcome feedback, DrugHarness induces action hypotheses with observable applicability conditions, recommended actions and supporting evidence. An independent review agent evaluates candidate skills derived from these hypotheses through source verification and, when needed, targeted execution trials. Admitted skills guide subsequent tasks and are revised through feedback. Evaluation on MolBench and SMDD-Bench demonstrates capabilities in molecular screening, optimization and constrained design. Without evolution, DrugHarness exceeds reported MolClaw-CC results by 10.8 percentage points in affinity-comparison accuracy and 55% in mean top-three active hits on MolBench. Retrospective evaluation on skill-development tasks shows that evolution increases Qwen's affinity-comparison accuracy from 62.2% to 81.1% and optimization success from 79.5% to 87.2%. In a prospective HPK1 study, DrugHarness achieves a more favorable median docking score than Biomni among system-specific candidates. A synthesized compound achieves a cellular IC50 of 14.19 nM, a 2.64-fold potency improvement over the best input reference, with activity competitive with two selected compounds designed by experienced medicinal chemists. These results support conditional skill evolution for scientific agents and demonstrate a workflow connecting computational drug design with experimental validation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Generative molecule evolution using 3D pharmacophore for efficient Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20130v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent breakthroughs in generative models have shown great promise for drug discovery, yet Structure-Based Drug Design (SBDD) faces two critical challenges: the scarcity of high-quality protein-ligand complex data and the practical synthesizability of generated molecules. While ligand-based approaches benefit from billion-scale small molecule datasets, they often lack the structural context of the target protein. To address these dual challenges of data scarcity and synthetic inaccessibility, we propose MEVO, a generative molecular evolutionary framework that operates within a synthesizable chemical space. MEVO introduces a reaction-aware generative model trained on reaction routes constructed by applying predefined reaction templates to Enamine Building Blocks. The model jointly generates molecules and corresponding synthesis sketches consistent with these templates. By integrating this generative capability into a pocket-aware evolutionary strategy, MEVO iteratively refines molecular structures within the synthesizable chemical space, guided by a hierarchical scoring function that combines fast approximation with rigorous Absolute Binding Free Energy Perturbation (ABFEP) calculations. This framework efficiently generates high-affinity, synthetically feasible binders for diverse protein targets. We validate MEVO's performance through rigorous ABFEP calculations, demonstrating its ability to design potent inhibitors for challenging targets such as KRAS$^{\textrm{G12D}}$, achieving affinities comparable to known active inhibitors. By integrating the data richness of ligand-based learning with the precision of structure-based optimization, MEVO offers a holistic solution that ensures both high binding affinity and synthetic accessibility, paving the way for more efficient and practical structure-based drug design.</p></details> |  |
| **[PMO-Dock: Benchmarking Docking, Specificity, and Generalization in Molecular Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19011v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Practical Molecular Optimization (PMO) benchmark standardized evaluation in molecular optimization, but it is built on simple property-based oracles that do not capture structure-based drug design. As the field has moved to docking-based objectives, evaluation practice has become unstandardized, and different methods report results on different docking tasks, often without a shared protocol for fair comparison or sample-efficiency constraints. To address this, we introduce Practical Molecular Optimization for Docking (PMO-Dock), a benchmark and protocol for docking-based optimization consisting of 25 tasks covering hit generation, lead optimization, and a new specificity task requiring strong on-target binding while penalizing off-target interactions. The protocol separates model development from final evaluation: validation tasks are used for hyperparameter tuning, and test tasks are held out for final evaluation and are not used during development. Evaluation on the test tasks is done under a strict oracle budget to reflect realistic drug-discovery settings. We benchmark four diverse high-performing methods spanning different optimization paradigms, Saturn (reinforcement learning), GenMol (discrete diffusion), Genetic-guided GFlowNet, and ChemLlama (LLM-based evolutionary search). Our results show no universally best method across tasks, additional analyses of hyperparameter transfer (cross-task and cross-category) and sensitivity show that transfer is often limited and varies by method, motivating method-dependent selection strategies. This benchmark provides a concrete standard for evaluating generalization to unseen tasks and sample efficiency under a limited test-time oracle budget.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Large Scale Investigation of Scaling Limits in Chemical Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13408v3)** | 2026-10-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chemical Language Models (CLMs) are increasingly used in de novo drug design, driven by recent growth in model scale, compute, and dataset size. By fitting IsoFLOP profiles, we establish clear scaling trends in pretraining loss, but find that these improvements do not translate into comparable gains in goal-directed molecular design. Layer-wise probing and sparse autoencoder analysis reveal continued development of chemical representations: chemical syntax saturates early, while semantic properties emerge more slowly and become increasingly accessible with further training and model scale. These representational gains coexist with diminishing improvements in goal-directed generation under the evaluated protocols and oracle budgets. Our resulting suite of models, NovoMolGen, achieves state-of-the-art results, outperforming prior CLMs and specialized generative models in goal-directed molecular generation across drug discovery tasks. These findings expose a disconnect between chemical representation learning and downstream molecular design, motivating the development of pretraining paradigms that more directly learn chemical semantics.</p></details> |  |
| **[One Sequence, Many Decodings: CAGenMol-2 Recasts Drug Design as Masked Molecular Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34301v1)** | 2026-09-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Drug design couples property evaluation, conditional generation, structure-based design, and local optimization, yet machine learning systems typically address these capabilities with separate task-specific models. | **[PocketVE: Stable and Property-Guided Structure-Based Drug Design with Variance-Exploding Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08101v3)** | 2026-09-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Protein-conditioned 3D molecule generation is a central challenge in structure-based drug design, requiring a balance between pocket compatibility, molecular properties, and physical geometry. Specifically, PocketVE combines an EDM-style training and sampling setup for 3D denoising, classifier-free guidance for multi-property steering, and adaptive protein perturbation as a training-time pocket regularizer.On the CrossDocked2020 benchmark under GenBench3D, PocketVE improves Valid$_{3\text{D}}$ from 58.6 to 80.6 and reduces strain energy from 457.4 to 127.9 relative to its guided TAGMol architectural parent; relative to TargetDiff, it attains comparable Valid$_{3\text{D}}$ with lower strain energy (127.9 vs.\ 306.0), while retaining competitive docking and molecular-property scores under moderate guidance. | **[A Systematic Evaluation of Molecule Generation Models for De Novo Drug Design: From Benchmarks to Practical Insights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10099v1)** | 2026-09-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Molecule generation has emerged as a powerful computational tool for de novo drug design, enabling the exploration of chemical space beyond the limits of conventional virtual screening. The field has progressed rapidly, driven by advances in molecular representations, generative architectures, and target-aware modeling strategies. However, existing reviews typically address specific model families or application scenarios in isolation, rather than offering an integrated perspective on how these components collectively form a coherent generation workflow. In this review, we present a comprehensive evaluation of molecule generation models for de novo drug design, covering 82 methods across five deep generative frameworks, including recurrent neural network (RNN)- and Transformer-based models, variational autoencoders (VAEs), generative adversarial networks (GANs), flow-based models, and diffusion models. We first summarize widely used benchmarks and molecular representations, and then examine the methodological principles underlying both general and pocket-conditioned generation. A central contribution of this work is a systematic synthesis and comparative analysis of reported performance across commonly used benchmarks and evaluation metrics. We also summarize representative experimentally validated case studies. Looking ahead, we discuss future directions in standardized 3D data, interaction-aware generation, receptor flexibility, and multi-objective molecular design, with the aim of improving the reliability and experimental relevance of molecule generation. All collected benchmark resources, evaluation metrics, and model references are provided in a publicly accessible repository at https://github.com/JacklinGroup/molecule-generation-review.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This document is the unedited Author's version of a Submitted Manuscript subsequently accepted for publication in [Journal of Chemical Information and Modeling]. To access the final published article</p></details> |
| **[FuseDiff: Symmetry-Preserving Joint Diffusion for Dual-Target Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05567v2)** | 2026-09-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dual-target structure-based drug design aims to generate a single ligand together with two pocket-specific binding poses, each compatible with a corresponding target pocket, enabling polypharmacological therapies with improved efficacy and reduced resistance. | **[NEAT-POCKET: Pocket-Conditioned Autoregressive 3D Molecular Generation with a Neighborhood-Guided Set Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05097v1)** | 2026-09-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI-driven de novo molecular design offers a promising route to accelerate early-stage drug discovery by generating novel ligands directly within target protein binding pockets. We compose the reward from a conditional value-at-risk (CVaR), a worst-case score rule, and an ambiguity radius expressing distrust in the stated weights. To address this, we introduce ChemCoTBench, a reasoning framework that bridges molecular structure understanding with arithmetic-inspired operations, including addition, deletion, and substitution, to formalize chemical problem-solving into transparent, step-by-step workflows. By treating molecular transformations as modular "chemical operations", the framework enables slow-thinking reasoning, mirroring the logic of mathematical proofs while grounding solutions in real-world chemical constraints. We evaluate models on two high-impact tasks: Molecular Property Optimization and Chemical Reaction Prediction. These tasks mirror real-world challenges while providing structured evaluability. By providing annotated datasets, a reasoning taxonomy, and baseline evaluations, ChemCoTBench bridges the gap between abstract reasoning methods and practical chemical discovery, establishing a foundation for advancing LLMs as tools for AI-driven scientific innovation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2025 Dataset Track, 22 pages, 10 figures</p></details> |
| **[ReACT-Drug: Reaction-Template Guided Reinforcement Learning for de novo Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.20958v1)** | 2025-12-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>De novo drug design is a crucial component of modern drug development, yet navigating the vast chemical space to find synthetically accessible, high-affinity candidates remains a significant challenge. Reinforcement Learning (RL) enhances this process by enabling multi-objective optimization and exploration of novel chemical space - capabilities that traditional supervised learning methods lack. In this work, we introduce \textbf{ReACT-Drug}, a fully integrated, target-agnostic molecular design framework based on Reinforcement Learning. Unlike models requiring target-specific fine-tuning, ReACT-Drug utilizes a generalist approach by leveraging ESM-2 protein embeddings to identify similar proteins for a given target from a knowledge base such as Protein Data Base (PDB). Thereafter, the known drug ligands corresponding to such proteins are decomposed to initialize a fragment-based search space, biasing the agent towards biologically relevant subspaces. For each such fragment, the pipeline employs a Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) agent guiding a ChemBERTa-encoded molecule through a dynamic action space of chemically valid, reaction-template-based transformations. This results in the generation of \textit{de novo} drug candidates with competitive binding affinities and high synthetic accessibility, while ensuring 100\% chemical validity and novelty as per MOSES benchmarking. This architecture highlights the potential of integrating structural biology, deep representation learning, and chemical synthesis rules to automate and accelerate rational drug design. The dataset and code are available at https://github.com/YadunandanRaman/ReACT-Drug/.</p></details> |  |

## multi-objective optimization
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[SliMOO: Interpretable Multi-Objective Evolutionary Search for LLM Depth Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27812v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models (LLMs) grow more powerful, their scale makes them costly to serve. Depth pruning offers a practical route to efficiency by directly reducing memory and latency without hardware-specific support. However, we observe that transformer layers exhibit markedly different task-dependent layer contributions, whereas existing depth-pruning methods ignore this heterogeneity and thus tend to optimize for a narrow domain or benchmark rather than preserve broad capability. This suggests that LLM depth pruning should be formulated as a multi-objective optimization problem rather than a single-score ranking problem. We propose SliMOO, an interpretable multi-objective evolutionary search framework for LLM depth pruning. SliMOO first uses single-layer removal probing to construct a task-layer importance map, which provides an interpretable view of task-shared and task-specific layers and serves as a search prior. It then performs a Pareto-aware evolutionary search for pruning masks, where candidates are generated under the guidance of the task-layer prior, scored by their deviation from the dense model on each task, and retained through NSGA-II environmental selection. Across the Llama-3.1 and Qwen3 model families, SliMOO consistently achieves stronger multi-task trade-offs than rule-based, greedy, and scalarized baselines. For example, on Llama-3.1-70B at 25% sparsity, it improves the math-domain average from 24.33% to 34.40% and the code-domain average from 35.03% to 39.14%. Code will be made available at https://github.com/AMD-AGI/SliMOO.</p></details> |  |
| **[Jacobian Descent for Multi-Objective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.16232v4)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many optimization problems require balancing multiple conflicting objectives. To generalize gradient descent, which is limited to single-objective optimization, we introduce Jacobian descent (JD) and its stochastic variants. This algorithm iteratively updates parameters using the Jacobian matrix of a vector-valued objective function, in which each row is the gradient of an individual objective. While several methods to combine gradients already exist in the literature, they generally struggle when the objectives conflict. In contrast, we propose projecting gradients to fully resolve conflict. We prove convergence to the Pareto front in the smooth convex case with this approach, a stronger guarantee than the usual convergence to a weakly Pareto stationary point. Our method also enables instance-wise risk minimization (IWRM), a novel learning paradigm in which the loss of each training example is considered a separate objective. Early experimentation on image classification shows promising results for IWRM. Lastly, we provide an efficient implementation of JD using the Gramian of the Jacobian matrix to drastically reduce memory requirements.</p></details> | <details><summary>53 pa...</summary><p>53 pages, 14 figures, Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (poster)</p></details> |
| **[Communication-Efficient Federated Domain Generalization in Edge Networks via Dynamic Prototype Regulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25810v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated learning (FL) is a distributed paradigm for Internet of Things (IoT) edge networks. However, deploying Vision-Language Models (VLMs) over wireless edge networks presents distinct challenges: large model parameters increase uplink transmission latency over bandwidth-constrained fading channels, and non-independent and identically distributed (Non-IID) data exacerbates client drift. Existing feature alignment solutions often introduce auxiliary computations that consume time slots allocated for wireless parameter transmission, increasing the likelihood of straggler dropouts under system delay constraints. To address these issues, we propose DPGR-GCAM, a communication-efficient federated domain generalization framework. By employing a dual-parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) architecture, we design a channel-aware tiered transmission mechanism that adaptively truncates payloads to maximize node survival rates under fading conditions. To mitigate client drift, we introduce the Gradient-Conflict Aware Modulation (GCAM) mechanism. GCAM operates within the low-dimensional feature manifold to dynamically resolve multi-objective optimization conflicts with minimal edge computation latency, thereby reducing computation-induced network outages. Experiments demonstrate that DPGR-GCAM improves cross-domain generalization and sustains convergence under network fading scenarios with a 10\% client participation rate.</p></details> |  |
| **[Python Tool for Visualizing Variability of Pareto Fronts over Multiple Runs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.08852v2)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hyperparameter optimization is crucial to achieving high performance in deep learning. On top of the performance, other criteria such as inference time or memory requirement often need to be optimized due to some practical reasons. This motivates research on multi-objective optimization (MOO). However, Pareto fronts of MOO methods are often shown without considering the variability caused by random seeds and this makes the performance stability evaluation difficult. Although there is a concept named \emph{empirical attainment surface} to enable the visualization with uncertainty over multiple runs, there is no major Python package for empirical attainment surface. We, therefore, develop a Python package for this purpose and describe the usage. The package is available at https://github.com/nabenabe0928/empirical-attainment-func. OptunaHub now provides our implementation at https://hub.optuna.org/visualization/plot_empirical_attainment_surface/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Submi...</summary><p>Submitted to AutoML workshop</p></details> |
| **[On the possibilities and limitations of LLM-based multi-agent systems for multi-criteria decision-making in healthcare operations management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23673v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-criteria models for healthcare operations need weights that express how stakeholders trade off patient waiting time, provider workload, and operating cost, but these weights are difficult to quantify. Teams of large language model (LLM) agents, each prompted to represent a stakeholder, have been used to supply them. We propose a framework for assessing the reliability of LLM-generated preferences as inputs to multi-objective optimization and quantifying how their uncertainty affects decisions. We study explicit preferences by asking agents to rate the importance of the criteria in an outpatient appointment scheduling problem, and implicit preferences by inferring trade-offs from the agents' pairwise choices between emergency department configurations. The computational experiments show that the explicit preferences respond to the clinic's operating conditions and differ across roles, but deliberation among the agents can erase these differences, whereas simple averaging preserves them. The pairwise choices used to reveal the agents' implicit preferences depend largely on which option is presented first, and we find limited evidence that a statistical model using contextual information alongside presentation order predicts the agents' choices on new comparison pairs better than a model using presentation order alone. We then formulate robust weighted-sum optimization models to account for preference uncertainty. In most cases, these models select different schedules or configurations from those obtained using average preference weights. These results indicate that LLM-generated preferences need an audit of this kind before they are used as optimization weights in healthcare decision support.</p></details> |  |
| **[ParetoChat: An LLM-Based Interface for Interactive Multi-Objective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23062v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-objective optimization models are often deployed in settings where decision makers (DMs) must balance some conflicting criteria. In practice, however, stakeholders must translate their qualitative preferences between different criteria into numerical weights, priorities, or reference points required by optimization methods, which can be difficult to specify and interpret. This paper proposes \emph{ParetoChat}, an LLM (large language model)-based expert-agent interface for interactive multi-objective optimization. The agent translates natural-language user input into structured scalarization parameters, invokes an optimization system, presents candidate solutions, and iteratively elicits feedback. We describe a multi-agent architecture that separates input processing, multi-criteria decision-making (MCDM) expertise, solution presentation, and feedback processing. We then present a simulation study using an LLM-based DM agent and benchmark ParetoChat against interactive methods implemented in DESDEO, including reference-point and NIMBUS methods. The computational experiments on multi-objective binary knapsack and set-partitioning instances show that ParetoChat can guide the search efficiently toward solutions that match the DM's preferences. An application to historical courier-order assignment instances illustrates how users can explore objective extremes and intermediate trade-offs through natural-language requests. The results indicate that LLM agents may provide a practical interaction layer between business stakeholders and optimization tools.</p></details> |  |
| **[Progressive Lower Bound Evaluation for Urban Road Renovation Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15554v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Urban infrastructure degrades over time, necessitating periodic renovation to maintain functionality and safety. When renovation is delayed beyond the infrastructure's remaining lifespan, costly emergency interventions become necessary to prevent failure. Decision makers must therefore balance expected emergency intervention costs against traffic congestion impacts. We formalize this trade-off as a road network maintenance scheduling problem with uncertain deadlines, which presents optimization challenges including computationally expensive evaluation and an exponentially growing solution space. We formulate the problem as a bi-level multi-objective optimization problem that explicitly accounts for uncertain infrastructure lifespans through probabilistic failure models. We develop a progressive lower bound evaluation method: candidate schedules receive quick, optimistic estimates of their traffic impact from machine learning based surrogate models, and these estimates are refined by exact traffic simulation only for promising candidates, so that full evaluation is reserved almost exclusively for schedules that can advance the Pareto front. Embedded in a multi-objective genetic algorithm, this evaluation strategy improves solution quality by enabling many more iterations within a fixed computational budget. We demonstrate the method's effectiveness and scalability on substantially larger problem instances than previously addressed in the literature: 76 and 80 projects on the Sioux Falls and Anaheim networks, respectively. Under an identical 24-hour computational budget across 30 random seeds, the method completes up to 87 times as many optimization iterations as standard NSGA-II and achieves statistically significant improvements across multiple performance metrics over both standard NSGA-II and a surrogate-assisted baseline.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 12 figures. Accepted manuscript of the article published in Computer-Aided Civil and Infrastructure Engineering, https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cacaie.2026.100174</p></details> |
| **[In-Context Preference-Conditioned Pareto Set Learning from Limited Offline Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21736v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-objective optimization involves diverse preferences over conflicting objectives. Rather than returning a finite set of trade-off solutions, preference-conditioned Pareto set learning seeks to map a specified preference directly to a corresponding Pareto optimal solution. Yet in many real-world scenarios, optimization must proceed offline without further objective evaluations, leaving only a limited static dataset that sparsely covers the decision and objective spaces. Existing methods either optimize separately for each preference or train a problem-specific Pareto solution model, requiring repeated search across preferences or repeated training across problems. We introduce in-context preference-conditioned Pareto set learning (ICPSL), a framework that amortizes both preference-wise solution search and problem-specific model construction. ICPSL pretrains a foundation-style hypernetwork on a large and diverse set of synthetic prior data. Given the offline observations of an unseen problem, the pretrained hypernetwork learns to generate a problem-specific, preference-conditioned Pareto solution model in a single forward pass. The generated model can then be reused to predict solutions for arbitrary preferences without problem-specific training or preference-wise search. Experiments on synthetic and real-world problems show that ICPSL produces high-quality Pareto solutions while providing substantial speedups in problem-specific solution-model construction.</p></details> |  |
| **[LOADBench: A Large Open Analog Dataset and Benchmark for Machine Learning in IC Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21591v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Machine learning (ML) for analog IC design is constrained by limited open data and the absence of standardized evaluation: results reported in different papers are typically obtained on bespoke, often closed, datasets and with ad-hoc metrics, leaving comparisons across methods unreliable. We address both gaps with a large opensource dataset and a suite of standardized benchmarks. The dataset contains more than 100 operational amplifier (opamp) topologies (1-, 2-, and 3-stage) in the SkyWater130 PDK, each shipped with structural and functional annotations, topology-specific sizing constraints (symmetry and sizing rules), an Ngspice testbench, and a multi-objective NSGA-II sizing run producing around 103 Pareto-optimal and 106 intermediate sizing-performance pairs per topology. On top of this data we define six benchmark tasks with precise protocols: inverse sizing, topology selection, sub-circuit classification, structural circuit generation, Pareto-membership prediction, and multi-objective optimization. Thus, the data presented here constitutes the most comprehensive dataset for ML development in over four decades of analog IC design automation. The dataset, ground-truth annotations, and reference implementations of the metric-defining routines are released under an open license, enabling reproducible and directly comparable evaluation across heterogeneous analog design automation methods.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 6 figures, Conference: MLCAD '26: 8th ACM/IEEE International Symposium on Machine Learning for CAD, September 7 to 9, 2026, Jeju, South Korea</p></details> |
| **[First Mathematical Runtime Analyses of Evolutionary Multitask Multi-Objective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21311v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>To facilitate the solving of complex problems, evolutionary multitask optimization is regarded as a promising technique due to its capacity for cross-task knowledge transfer. We analyze how the representative multiobjective multifactorial evolutionary algorithm (MO-MFEA) covers the Pareto fronts of two bi-objective problems simultaneously. | **[When the implementation matters more than the algorithm: NSGA-II and other multi-objective optimizers in engineering design tools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22397v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Engineering design tools increasingly embed optimizers as ready-made components that users select by algorithm name, while operator settings and bound, integer and duplicate handling stay fixed inside each tool. Eighteen configurations were run at 5,000 evaluations under three population allocations, with statistics fixed in advance. On ZDT test functions the same NSGA-II implementations differed up to 470-fold in IGD. An ablation in one open-source NSGA-II reproduced the behaviour of one tool from its documented settings but not the gaps of the other two, so documented parameters do not identify an implementation. Counting the budget in wall-clock time reversed the ranking of the model-based optimizer, and reporting the final population instead of the archive understated some configurations by up to a third; most differences fell below the surrogate's error. For optimizers embedded in design-support systems, the implementation is part of the method; a reporting checklist is derived: implementation and version, effective settings, budget in time and evaluations, and archive-based indicators.</p></details> | <details><summary>29 pa...</summary><p>29 pages, 8 figures, 5 tables. Per-run data, emulator models and analysis scripts: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23157761</p></details> |
| **[Enabling Validation for Robust Few-Shot Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04713v4)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A key challenge in Few-Shot Recognition (FSR) is the scarcity of labeled task-specific data for training. Contemporary methods mitigate this challenge by exploiting pretrained Vision-Language Models (VLMs) for their learned transferable features. To resolve this dilemma, we formulate validation as a multi-objective optimization (MOO) problem between performance gains on the few-shot ID data and the performance preservation on the retrieved data. We introduce a simple yet effective validation strategy by choosing an operating point on the Pareto front of the MOO problem. This strategy enables hyperparameter tuning, which had been unrealistically done by using test data, thereby mitigating overfitting and improving generalization. | **[Large Language Models for Model-Based Robot Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33423v3)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) can contribute useful engineering knowledge to robot design, but directly generated designs may rely on implicit assumptions and provide no guarantees of feasibility or optimality. The model can then be updated with additional engineering, manufacturer, or system-specific information before formal multi-objective optimization provides feasibility and Pareto-optimality guarantees with respect to the finalized model and specified design space. Comparisons with an independently developed expert model and successive stages of model refinement further showed that changes in modeling assumptions substantially altered the predicted feasible and Pareto-optimal design sets. Together, these results show that using LLMs to construct explicit engineering models makes the underlying design choices available for inspection and revision before those assumptions determine the optimized designs. | **[When Rewards Disagree: Reward Weighting for Token-Efficient Video Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.04415v4)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human perception interprets video dynamics by constructing structured mental representations of entities, actions, and temporal boundaries before engaging in high-level reasoning. However, post-training a model to extract structured evidence while reasoning introduces a severe multi-objective optimization bottleneck, as candidate rollouts must simultaneously satisfy schema compliance, task accuracy, and reasoning budget. | **[SURF: Steering the Scalarization Weight to Uniformly Traverse the Pareto Front](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20619v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scalarization is widely used in multi-objective optimization owing to its simplicity and scalability. Building on this insight, we propose SURF (Sampling Uniformly along the Pareto Front). For structured problems, including bi-objective bandits, we derive analytical expressions for this CDF map and the resulting PF-aware weight sampling rule. | **[Scheduling in Cloud,Fog, and Edge Computing: A Comparative Analysis and Decision Framework for Technique Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13830v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The increasing heterogeneity and dynamism of cloud, fog, and edge computing make scheduling a challenging problem involving diverse resource capabilities, workload characteristics, latency and energy constraints, and competing optimization objectives. Studies published between 2020 and 2026 are analyzed according to scheduling granularity, computing environment, optimization objectives, constraints, performance metrics, and evaluation tools. The analysis highlights trade-offs among computational efficiency, optimization capability, adaptability, and implementation complexity across technique families. Heuristic methods offer low computational overhead for relatively stable scheduling conditions, whereas metaheuristic methods are suited to complex and multi-objective optimization problems. Learning-based methods provide greater adaptability to dynamic and uncertain environments but introduce data, training, inference, and convergence requirements. Hybrid methods combine complementary capabilities but generally increase computational and implementation complexity. Based on these findings, a decision framework is developed to guide technique selection according to workload characteristics, environmental conditions, optimization objectives, resource constraints, and computational requirements.</p></details> |  |

| **[PriMoRetarget: Humanoid Motion Transfer Through Prioritized Motion Retargeting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12642v1)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Motion retargeting is a critical preprocessing step for transferring human motion data to humanoid robots. | **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

These results demonstrate that tokenization introduces a critical inductive bias and that incorporating evolutionary information yields more biologically meaningful and interpretable sequence representations. Because $\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{DNA}}$ is rigid monoidal, it shares the categorical backbone of pregroup grammars and the DisCoCat framework for compositional semantics: a strong monoidal functor from a grammatical category to $\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{DNA}}$ maps grammatical reductions to Watson-Crick base pairing and sentence meanings to secondary structures. | **[Weight Placement Is Not Enough: Synchronization in Multi-Node CPU Decoding of Ternary LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28620v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Ternary LLMs read only a few gigabytes per decoded token, yet on dual-socket servers with four NUMA nodes the fastest ternary runtime we found uses less than half of the memory bandwidth. Placing each weight on the node of the thread that reads it is necessary but not enough: in a decode engine with node-local weights that reuses ik_llama.cpp's kernels, threads on two generations of Xeon servers still spend 20-48% of each token waiting at synchronization points. Per-thread barrier traces show that most of the wait is for the slowest threads of each node; the barriers' own latency (microseconds each, but up to 12% of the token for the smallest model) and serial per-node work are smaller parts. On identical kernels, a Megatron-style column split beats a row-owned split without cross-node reductions in every configuration we measured, and isolating the number of barriers shows that most of its gain comes from its threads waiting less for each other and for their node leaders, and only a minority from having fewer barriers. Where decoding is DRAM-bound, the resulting engine is 1.5-2.1x faster than ik_llama.cpp with all cores and 1.5-1.9x faster than ik_llama.cpp at its fastest thread count (3.2x and 2.8x for a small model that runs from the L3 cache), at the same accuracy and with less energy per token.</p></details> | 15 pages, 9 figures |

Across five seeded evaluation partitions, classical selectors generally achieve higher predictive performance than the tested 7B LLM/RL selectors, including under a pre-specified ~33% feature budget with strict train-only classical fitting, while the 7B selectors retain compact subsets. A cardinality-matched semantic sensitivity experiment shows that, for Zero-shot Qwen-7B, informative feature names improve regression performance over anonymized and shuffled alternatives (+0.1964 and +0.1606 mean R^2), whereas aggregate classification differences are smaller (+0.0159 and +0.0084 ROC-AUC) and dataset-dependent. | **[When Dynamic Scaling Fails: A Scale-State Feedback Governs Recurrent-State Quantization in Linear-Attention and SSM LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15139v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Linear-attention and state-space LLMs carry long context in a fixed-size recurrent state, and quantizing that state is the natural way to cut its cost during long decode. INT4 that leaves synthetic recall perfectly intact at the start destroys it a thousand steps later. The mechanism is a feedback between the quantizer scale and the state it is derived from. Yoking that scale to a full-precision reference removes the resulting norm explosion entirely, yet recovers only 1.8 of the 5.4 nats lost, so numerical instability and functional degradation are separable failures. The remainder is resolution-dependent, and in our per-step setting every outlier-aware scheme we test loses to plain persistent scaling. Allocating bits across layers does help, but under article-disjoint splits a learned allocation collapses onto the trivial rule of protecting the first few layers. Run out to 32K, group-wise INT4 is worth no more than discarding the state. Contain the scale rather than locate outliers, and report the decode horizon at which a bit-width was certified.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 6 figures, 7 tables. | **[After the Attacker Leaves: Benchmarking Carryover Attacks on LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28425v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern LLM agents persist state across sessions via two channels: long-lived memory files silently prepended to the system prompt, and scheduled tasks waking the agent without user input. | **[MAS-Guard: Unified Guardrails from Individual Agents to Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28397v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic AI extends large language models (LLMs) from generating responses to planning and executing complex workflows, substantially expanding their utility while exposing applications to unsafe actions, information leakage, and adversarial manipulation. | **[BAIT: Can LLMs Find What Biases a Reader? Moreover, they over-predict triggers that come with easy surface cues, and most of the passages they mark for these triggers are not triggers, yet carry the trigger's surface cue far more often than random text. On this basis, the paper develops the notion of an \emph{epistemic audit}: a structured record of delegation, verification, provenance, and responsibility intended to make AI-assisted reasoning transparent and reviewable. On labeled benchmarks, peer scores align strongly with accuracy on TruthfulQA (Pearson r=0.904) and GSM8K (r=0.873). On TruthfulQA, the peer-score correlation is substantially higher than the self-score correlation (r=0.538). Code and dataset: https://github.com/caura-ai/caura-PeerRank</p></details> | <details><summary>Versi...</summary><p>Version 2.0 of PeerRank</p></details> |
| **[Hearsay or Fact? Five weighted estimates lower the share of responses at $\leq$5% or $\geq$95% in all seven, but in the two local models they also reduce s by 43% and 68%, whereas in the harnessed configurations the point estimates of the change lie between -3.7 and +0.5 points. Because $δ$-NLI annotates only direction, we make no claim about how large an update should be. The reported pool is the best of ten for both retrieval memories, which beat No-Memory in only one pool of ten (sign test p=0.011), and even the rulebook's apparent pool-immunity collapsed on five fresh draws. (2) No retrieval design tested exceeds token-matched filler. The collision-only merge ceiling is reached by a single 15-token caution instruction, and metrics that price caution erase the rulebook's lead. (3) Representational sophistication is nowhere robustly operative. (4) Baseline port quality silently inverts conclusions. Our central result is an impossibility: relational purpose violations form a 2-safety hyperproperty with no per-trace locus of blame, so a per-action monitor that never raises a false alarm detects none of them, independent of the underlying model or decision procedure; they become visible only under cross-execution monitoring, which none of the six guardrails we survey implements. | **[AllPath: Layered Verification for a Deployed, Self-Revising LLM Trading Agent under Human-Gated and Autonomous Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26662v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM trading agents are evaluated almost exclusively in backtests and autonomous benchmark harnesses. The agent verifies itself within fixed bounds: its nightly review has an iteration cap and no tools that can act. A human verifies judgment, through approval screens that show the current state of each proposal. We also compared two verification policies for the agent's self-revisions on the live system: two and a half weeks with every self-revision human-gated, then five weeks with reflection revisions auto-applied behind the same code gates, while the operator's chat channel stayed human-gated throughout. Each layer caught a different kind of failure. The code gates rejected no proposal; their role was keeping revisions consistent. The human rejected two proposals that passed every code check, both over a disagreement about the market, and later removed 11 of the 19 rules the agent had added on its own, at a median lag of eight days. The most instructive production incidents, such as strategies silently left unmonitored, passed every gate; the operator found them by hand, and the fix each time was an interface change that makes the condition visible. Behavioral ablations show that the scaffold establishes a tool-call prior; Transcoder decomposition then reveals that analysis verbs suppress this prior through features signaling that tool use is unnecessary, whereas execution verbs largely leave it intact. With one photo, two models had a near-zero intake bias only because they under-estimated the served energy and ignored uneaten food.
