The Touche 2026 lab at CLEF formalises this as a shared task on a Reddit-derived corpus that combines fallacy detection with argument-scheme classification. This paper describes our participation in all three subtasks (ST1 binary fallacy detection, ST2 eight-way fallacy classification, ST3 four-way argument-scheme classification) and both evaluation tracks. Our submitted systems are fine-tuned RoBERTa-large encoders with task-specific heads, augmented with a small batch of LLM-generated synthetic contrastive pairs; for ST2 and ST3 on the enhanced track we additionally added confidence-filtered pseudo-labelled examples from the unlabelled pool. A wider backbone comparison spanning additional encoders (DeBERTa-v3, ModernBERT) and an LLM track (four Qwen variants from 3B to 32B, zero-shot through CoT-LoRA) showed no validated benefit over RoBERTa-large at this corpus scale, with 5-fold variance wide enough that we call the comparison underpowered rather than settled. We also characterise how much of the enhanced track's lift reflects organiser-rewritten inputs carrying label information rather than genuine argument clarification. On the official Touche 2026 test set our systems ranked first on both base-track classification cells (ST2 and ST3) and second on both binary-detection (ST1) tracks, with the two enhanced-track classification cells topped by cascaded and multitask systems. We close with the rare-class limitation on ST3 and the most useful follow-ups. Code: https://github.com/psychias/fallacy_detection_clef_2026</p></details> | <details><summary>19 pa...</summary><p>19 pages. Working notes for Touche at CLEF 2026 (Jena, Germany)</p></details> |
| **[Test-Time Augmentation for LLMs: Where to Inject Diversity at Inference Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09351v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Aggregating several LLM responses to the same question can improve accuracy, but every method that does so must decide where the responses should differ. Established test-time scaling methods such as self-consistency vary only the reasoning path, resampling from a fixed input. The input modification offers several further options: the wording of the question can be rephrased, the spelling can be perturbed, and the accompanying images can be transformed. We study Test-Time Augmentation (TTA), which aggregates predictions across transformed versions of the input, and use it to ask where diversity is best injected. Comparing these options across six benchmarks at a matched number of inference calls, we find that where the diversity comes from matters more than how much of it there is. Meaning-preserving paraphrases of the input yield the largest accuracy gains (+1.80 pp over a single call) and reach peak accuracy with the fewest samples. Yet, they are not the most diverse: character-level noise makes the responses disagree far more often (37.8% versus 27.4%) while gaining less accuracy, and perturbing two modalities at once falls below the single-call baseline. Not all diversity is equally useful: perturbations that keep the meaning intact produce disagreement that aggregation can use, while those that corrupt it produce disagreement that is wrong. Overall, our results suggest that for mid-tier LLMs such as Claude Haiku, varying how a question is phrased is a better use of an inference call than varying the reasoning path. Our TTA implementation is available at github.com/aws-samples/sample-genai-reflection-for-bedrock.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published at: - NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on Linguistic Principles for Foundation Models - COLM 2026 Workshop on Efficient Reasoning</p></details> |
| **[Sequential Behavioral Watermarking for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11036v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based agents act through sequences of executable decisions, but their trajectories provide little evidence of which agent or policy produced them, making provenance, ownership, and unauthorized reuse difficult to establish from observed behavior alone. This motivates watermarking signals embedded directly into agent behavior rather than only into generated text, since text watermarking cannot capture the action-level decisions that define agent execution. Recent agent watermarking methods address this gap by moving the watermark from generated text to behavioral choices. However, by treating each action step as an independent trial, they overlook trajectory structure and become fragile when trajectories are perturbed, truncated, or observed without reliable alignment. We propose SeqWM, a sequential behavioral watermarking framework that embeds signals into history-conditioned transition patterns and verifies trajectories position-agnostically against random-key baselines. Experiments across diverse agent benchmarks and LLM backbones show that SeqWM consistently achieves reliable detection while preserving agent utility, and remains robust under trajectory corruption where round-indexed behavioral watermarks collapse.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 3 figures, preprint</p></details> |
| **[LLMs Prompted for Legal Context Refuse More: Overrefusal from Small On-Premises LLMs in Criminal Legal Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26211v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Confidentiality requirements increasingly push legal professionals toward small, on-device LLMs that keep case data in-house, used for everyday tasks such as summarization, translation, and reformulation. Following standard prompting guidance, a user naturally states their role (``I am a lawyer...''). We show that this innocuous habit has an unexpected cost: such authority framing can make the model selectively refuse legitimate requests, introducing bias through uneven access to assistance. We evaluate four modern small open-weight LLMs on benign legal-domain prompts to measure this over-refusal. Surprisingly, we find that authority-style prefixes (''I am legal counsel working for a national supreme court'', ''I'm a defense lawyer'') systematically increase refusal rates by 2--20x over the no-prefix baseline, while a known role-play jailbreak prefix shows mixed effects, sharply increasing refusals in some models and barely shifting them in others. The finding suggests that small on-prem deployable LLMs are unstable under contextual framings that a real institutional user might naturally introduce, and further investigation is essential to minimize opportunities for bias.</p></details> |  |
| **[CEMA-Router: Infrastructure-Aware Batch Routing for LLM Inference in Cloud-Edge Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26171v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLM) are increasingly integrated into pervasive applications spanning heterogeneous cloud-edge resources. These heterogeneous resources differ in computational capacity, energy efficiency, network conditions, and deployed model quality, creating trade-offs among response quality, monetary cost, energy consumption, and batch completion time when routing inference requests. Existing LLM routers typically make per-query routing decisions based primarily on model quality and token cost, whereas cloud-edge schedulers often overlook quality differences among deployed models. Consequently, these objectives remain insufficiently coordinated. We present CEMA-Router, an infrastructure-aware batch router for LLM inference in pervasive cloud-edge systems. CEMA-Router formulates batch routing as a multi-objective search problem that jointly minimizes model-quality loss, monetary cost, energy consumption, and straggler-dominated batch completion time. To efficiently explore the search space, it employs a routing-specific memetic algorithm, combining largest-request-first, load-aware seeding with probabilistic, evaluator-guided Lamarckian repair. Routing decisions are informed by deployment-specific node profiles, dynamic backlog estimates, and model-quality signals. We further implement CEMA-Router as an end-to-end system comprising a request manager, routing service, and profiler, supporting both real cloud-edge deployment and trace-driven simulation. We evaluate the system using RouterBench traces and GSM8K and MMLU workloads on a heterogeneous cloud-edge testbed. Compared with state-of-the-art methods, CEMA-Router reduces the weighted objective by up to 86% for GSM8K and 80% for MMLU, while reducing energy consumption by up to 33% and 19% and batch completion time by up to 27% and 30%, respectively.</p></details> |  |
| **[Reliable Before Profitable: QuantHarness for Governing Financial Strategy Development with LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26101v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents can turn natural-language financial research requests into executable and backtested strategies, but a successful run may still implement the wrong rule, use information unavailable at decision time, or obscure how repairs and candidate selection changed the strategy. Because these failures arise throughout development, final code and performance metrics alone are insufficient to establish either fidelity to the request or the validity of the development process. We therefore address the reliability of financial strategy development at the trajectory level: the system must control how intermediate outputs become downstream state and preserve the evidence needed to justify the terminal outcome. We introduce QuantHarness, a two-layer agent harness in which a finance-specific Workflow structures specification, data grounding, construction, testing, and review, while Harness Core enforces stage-specific information, action, and commitment boundaries. We also introduce FinRelBench, a benchmark that evaluates strategy-development reliability through execution success, requirement satisfaction, and runtime evidence. QuantHarness achieves the highest Task Success at 90.72\%, compared with 74.23\%--84.54\% for the comparison systems. Controlled stress tests show that its boundaries contain targeted violations, while structured run records improve evidence retrieval and support decision replay under the evaluated protocol.</p></details> | <details><summary>47 pa...</summary><p>47 pages, 2 figures, including appendices</p></details> |
| **[MX-Like Tensor Core for LLM Inference with NPU Integration via Hardware-Software Co-Design Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26083v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Block-scaled quantization formats such as OCP Microscaling (MX) and NVFP4 have been widely adopted as efficient data formats for LLM inference. However, the design space of these formats spanned by shared scale, weight, and activation format combinations has not been systematically explored, and the joint accuracy-silicon tradeoff across this space remains uncharacterized, leaving the true Pareto-optimal configurations for NPU-based edge LLM deployment an open question. To bridge this gap, we conduct a joint hardware-software (HW-SW) co-design exploration of the above underexplored MX-like design space with three innovations. 1) We define and systematically characterize the full 8-bit scale format family within the MX-like design space, jointly with asymmetric weight-activation precision, identifying configurations that outperform the MX and NVFP4-like (without per-tensor scaling, PTS) baselines. 2) We build a per-format specialized MAC datapath template that enables fair hardware cost evaluation across the space. 3) We develop an automated co-design flow, integrated into the open-source SNAX NPU platform, that enables joint HW-SW analysis across the design space. Overall, our co-design exploration reveals that scale format matters only under aggressive FP4 quantization, yielding a case-oriented design guideline for both scale format and asymmetric weight-activation precision. We further identify UE5M3 as a favorable accuracy-hardware tradeoff for the FP4 regime, reducing perplexity degradation by up to 25.3% relative to the NVFP4-like baseline, at a cost of only +0.06% area and +0.49% power overhead.</p></details> |  |
